**Holding and operative rule:** An asserted defect in the United States Attorney's appointment does not invalidate this appeal where an independently appointed assistant and the Department's own litigation authority support the notice and appeal. The assistant's section 542 appointment, section 516's departmental authority, the Solicitor General's criminal-appeal authority and Rule 3(c)'s absence of a required personal United States Attorney signature defeat Hilario's notice objection independently of Gil's appointment validity.

**Authority:** Justice Breyer's opinion supplies the Court's notice holding, which is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Stone-Zsela agrees with rejection of this objection without joining its ground.

**Controlling explanation:** Hilario treats the challenged United States Attorney as the only possible source of appellate authority. The notice also bears the assistant's signature, and section 542 makes the assistant's appointment independent. Section 516 reserves federal litigation to Justice Department officers under the Attorney General, subject to statutory exceptions; the Solicitor General controls the criminal-appeal decision under 28 C.F.R. 0.20(b). Rule 3(c) does not make the United States Attorney's personal signature indispensable. The alleged appointment defect therefore does not defeat this properly supported appeal. This conclusion concerns appellate authority alone; it establishes no independent authorization of the indictment and no later ratification.

#### The judicial appointment continues until the vacancy is filled

**Holding and operative rule:** Under the contemporaneous section 546(d), when the Attorney General's interim appointment expires after 120 days under section 546(c)(2), the district court may appoint a United States Attorney to serve until the vacancy is filled. Section 541's ordinary four-year term does not displace that express endpoint, and Gil's judicial appointment did not terminate merely because four years passed.

**Authority:** All nine Justices support the direct section 546(d) holding in the opinion of the Court.

**Controlling explanation:** Congress specified distinct appointment routes and stopping conditions. Section 546(c) limits the Attorney General's appointee to the earlier of qualification of a presidential appointee or 120 days; subsection (d) then permits judicial appointment until the vacancy is filled. Importing the ordinary four-year term into that route would replace its explicit duration. The concern that prolonged vacancies may delay nomination and confirmation is serious, but it does not authorize a court to rewrite the endpoint. The ordinary presidential appointment route and presidential removal under section 541(c) remain available throughout. Section 546(b)'s prohibition on an Attorney General appointment of a person whom the Senate rejected remains distinct; the Court neither extends nor eliminates it. The judicial appointment order must still be filed with the clerk under subsection (d); no filing defect is presented. Later amendments have no part in this construction.

#### Independently, section 541's holdover defeats automatic expiration under the proposed four-year reading

**Holding and operative rule:** Even assuming section 541(b)'s four-year language applies to the judicial appointee, the same subsection directs the United States Attorney to continue performing the office's duties until a successor is appointed and qualifies. An anniversary alone therefore does not terminate authority on Hilario's proposed reading when no successor has replaced Gil.

**Authority:** Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer join this independently sufficient statutory alternative in the Court's opinion. Stone-Zsela does not join this alternative ground; he joins the direct vacancy-endpoint holding and judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The broad introductory language on which Hilario relies cannot be read apart from its express continuation command. Applying the four-year clause would also require giving effect to the holdover clause, which prevents the claimed automatic cessation. This alternative does not expand the Attorney General's 120-day appointment power or replace section 546(d)'s separate endpoint. It supplies an additional answer to the particular asserted expiration, without making continued service a cure for an unconstitutional appointment.

#### Actual combined executive controls make this office inferior

**Holding and operative rule:** United States Attorneys, including this judicial appointee, are inferior officers under the actual statutory structure because the Attorney General directs their duties and litigation, may take or reassign cases, and the President retains removal. Substantial district-level prosecutorial duties and long tenure do not make the office independent of those combined controls; no single supervisory, review or removal power is held sufficient for every federal office.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer join the Court's combined-control rationale. Souter supports inferior status through the distinct ground in his concurrence.

**Controlling explanation:** Edmond requires examination of the office's actual statutory hierarchy, rather than treating significant duties or an interim title as decisive. Sections 516 and 519 place litigation and the prosecutor's duties under the Attorney General's direction; section 518 permits executive takeover or reassignment of a case. Section 541(c) gives removal to the President, not a general discharge power to the Attorney General. Together, these controls leave the United States Attorney's substantial authority subject to executive superiors. Long service does not withdraw any of those powers. Edmond's military-review arrangement is not a checklist transplanted to prosecution; the legal controls appropriate to this office do the work. Weiss preserves the distinction between lawful appointment and later assignment. The ordinary nomination route remains available, but the office's inferior status rests on its actual subordinate organization rather than an assumption that replacement will occur promptly.

#### Judicial selection does not transfer prosecution to the courts

**Holding and operative rule:** Congress may vest appointment of this inferior executive officer in the district court under the Appointments Clause's court-of-law option when selection gives the judges no charging, litigation, removal or supervisory authority over the officer's execution of duties. The court's later hearing of cases brought by its appointee and the absence of automatic statutory disqualification do not alone establish unconstitutional partiality or an impermissible merger of judicial and executive power.

**Authority:** All nine Justices join the Court's interbranch holding, including its distinct judicial-integrity analysis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Constitution expressly permits Congress to choose courts of law to appoint inferior officers. Morrison and Ex parte Siebold support that option within their holdings, while Morrison and Mistretta require attention to incompatibility and judicial integrity. Section 546(d) differs from Morrison's arrangement: appointment belongs to the district court as a court, and the statute does not automatically exclude appointing judges from later cases involving the appointee. Those features warrant consideration but do not give judges control of prosecutions or a continuing partisan interest in their outcome. Appointment selects the officer; execution remains subject to executive direction and presidential removal. Functional examination confirms that separation rather than substituting a balance of convenience for constitutional limits. No actual judicial direction, coercion or bias is established here. A supported claim of that kind remains governed by its own facts and ordinary disqualification law; this holding creates no exemption from those rules.

#### Prolonged tenure alone does not invalidate the appointment

**Holding and operative rule:** More than six years without presidential replacement does not by itself invalidate a judicial appointment that remains within section 546(d)'s vacancy condition and the office's constitutionally sufficient executive controls. Passage of time neither converts this regular inferior office into a principal office nor authorizes a new judicially imposed term limit.

**Authority:** All nine Justices support rejection of the duration-only as-applied challenge; Souter reaches the classification premise through his separate fuller inquiry.

**Controlling explanation:** The challenge concerns a regular office whose powers remain subordinate, not an independent principal office temporarily occupied by an acting inferior officer. The vacancy endpoint, presidential removal and ordinary appointment process continue to operate after six years. Institutional concern about delay cannot erase those controls or amend the statutory duration. This conclusion neither excuses a genuine change in the office's legal control nor validates an independently defective act. Ryder preserves meaningful relief for qualifying timely appointment defects; it does not make longevity a cure or automatically require dismissal of an indictment issued by a prosecutor. Because the appointment is valid on the presented grounds, the Government's preserved argument against dismissal even assuming invalidity is unnecessary to decide. No independent indictment authorization, ratification or harmlessness finding is made.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Weiss preserves the distinction between appointment and assignment; its commissioned-officer holding does not itself authorize this separate office.
- Edmond's actual combined-control inquiry is applied to the prosecutorial hierarchy. The Court adopts no universal single-factor classification rule.
- Morrison and Ex parte Siebold support Congress's court-appointment option within their scope; Morrison and Mistretta also require the distinct judicial-integrity inquiry applied here.
- Ryder retains meaningful relief for a qualifying timely substantial appointment defect. It supplies neither a long-service cure nor automatic dismissal of a prosecutor's indictment; no defect requiring its remedy is established.

### Shafer v. South Carolina — 2001-03-20

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Shafer_v_South_Carolina_merits_2001-03-20.md#holdings), [precedent treatment](../records/Shafer_v_South_Carolina_merits_2001-03-20.md#precedent-treatment), and [current-law effect](../records/Shafer_v_South_Carolina_merits_2001-03-20.md#law-after-decision).

#### The jury's actual sentencing alternatives govern the information right

**Holding and operative rule:** When the State places future dangerousness in issue and a capital jury, after finding the required statutory aggravator, can choose only death or life without ordinary parole, due process requires an effective opportunity to explain that ineligibility through accurate defense presentation or an adequate instruction. A lesser sentence available only to the judge if the jury finds no aggravator does not defeat that protection at the later jury sentencing choice; the rule governs this staged procedure and materially equivalent choices.

**Authority:** Justice Souter's opinion of the Court, joined throughout by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies seven direct votes for this and the following two holdings. No combination of separate rationales is necessary.

**Controlling explanation:** The sentencing alternatives matter when the jury exercises the choice between death and continued life. Under section 16-3-20, a jury that has not found an aggravator performs a factual task and cannot recommend a sentence. If no aggravator is found, the judge becomes the sentencer. Once the aggravator is found, the jury has no thirty-year option. The State's reliance on the statute's complete list of possible punishments therefore mistakes a conditional judicial alternative for an alternative available at the decision Simmons protects.

Simmons supplies the right to answer a State future-danger case with the legally accurate noncapital alternative. Gardner supplies the opportunity to answer material capital-sentencing information, and Skipper protects a defendant's response to the State's future-danger premise. The protection cannot depend on whether the same two sentencing choices follow a preliminary factual determination. Ramdass likewise requires attention to actual legal consequences and a truthful account of any genuinely conditional consequence. Here life without ordinary parole is the actual alternative after the aggravator finding, not an anticipated conviction or uncertain future event.

The preliminary aggravator inquiry itself does not involve the sentencing discretion addressed by this rule. The right still requires a State future-danger case and a legally accurate ineligibility premise. The Court rejects the categorical structural bar without finding that the dangerousness predicate was satisfied in this trial.

#### The combined presentation was inadequate if future dangerousness was in issue

**Holding and operative rule:** If the prosecution put future dangerousness in issue, the repeated until-death definitions and permitted natural-life defense argument did not provide an effective informational opportunity in this case when considered together with the refusal of the requested statutory parole explanation and the supplemental direction excluding parole eligibility or ineligibility after the jury's questions. This determination concerns the legal adequacy of the complete presentation, requires no particular words, and makes no finding about any juror's actual belief.

**Authority:** The seven-Justice Court opinion identified above controls this complete, conditional adequacy determination.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court considers what counsel was allowed to say as well as what the judge said. This was not merely an undefined reference to life imprisonment: the judge repeatedly described confinement until death, and counsel argued that Shafer would spend his natural life in prison and never go home. Those explanations support the State's argument that the substance of ineligibility was conveyed. Simmons, however, requires an effective opportunity to use the accurate legal answer, not simply the presence of accurate duration language somewhere in the presentation.

After refusing the requested express statutory explanation, the court answered the parole inquiries by repeating the duration definition while placing both parole eligibility and ineligibility outside the jury's consideration. Taken as a whole, the presentation did not make ordinary-parole ineligibility available as the answer to a future-danger case. The questions referred generally to a murder conviction and do not establish what the jurors thought about a life sentence. Their generality nevertheless does not answer the legal question the combined presentation left unavailable at the jury's actual death-or-life choice.

The conclusion rests on that combination, not the refusal alone, the supplemental direction alone, or a requirement to use the word parole. Accurate effective defense presentation or an equivalent adequate judicial explanation remains sufficient. Ramos permits truthful release-related information; it does not require a guarantee against clemency or later legislation, and neither does this decision. The State remains free to present truthful evidence of danger within prison. Whether the State raised future dangerousness and whether an established violation affected death selection remain separate inquiries.

#### The remand returns dangerousness and error effect, not informational adequacy

**Holding and operative rule:** The state court must determine on the trial record whether the prosecution's evidence or argument placed future dangerousness in issue; if it did, informational inadequacy is settled and the State must prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the error did not contribute to the selection of death. If the State fails that burden, a new lawful penalty proceeding is required if it seeks death, or a lawful noncapital sentence may be imposed; if future dangerousness was not in issue, this Simmons due-process claim alone establishes no violation.

**Authority:** The same seven Justices join the Court's remand and its governing standard in full.

**Controlling explanation:** The South Carolina Supreme Court's categorical view of the sentencing scheme prevented it from deciding whether the prosecution raised future danger. The evidence, the purpose for which it was offered, the prosecutor's argument and the jury's questions must be assessed together. No prescribed phrase is necessary to raise future dangerousness, and circumstantial or qualitative proof cannot be disregarded merely because the prosecutor did not use that phrase. The jury's questions alone establish neither the predicate nor its absence. The state court must perform that record-based inquiry rather than return to the rejected structural bar.

If the predicate exists, the Court's second holding establishes the informational error. Chapman supplies the direct-review burden: the State must establish beyond a reasonable doubt that the error did not contribute to death selection. Simmons applies that burden to this kind of error and requires a lawful new penalty proceeding if the State cannot carry it and continues to seek death. Simmons's finding of harmfulness on its own record does not resolve the effect of this different presentation. The error is not structural and neither harmlessness nor harmfulness is found here.

Ramdass's distinct federal habeas conditions do not govern this direct review. The remaining predicate and effect determinations are ordinary work on remand under settled rules, not an invitation to decide adequacy again. No immediate new hearing, sentence substitution or sentence vacatur follows from the present judgment. Guilt and the separate sentences are unaffected.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The broader instruction right when the State has not raised future danger is not decided because the federal question resolved here is the conditional Simmons protection. No independently sufficient Eighth Amendment instruction ground is adopted. The actual dangerousness predicate and, if necessary, error effect await state adjudication on the existing record; these are application and remedial work under the holdings, not gaps in the legal adequacy rule.

- **Simmons v. South Carolina, 512 U.S. 154 (1994):** Applied to the actual death-or-life choice in a staged sentencing procedure and to the complete instructional presentation. Its effective-opportunity rule, alternative means of explanation and direct-review remedy remain controlling. Its own harmfulness finding is confined to its record.
- **Gardner v. Florida, 430 U.S. 349 (1977):** Its opportunity to answer material capital-sentencing information supplies the due-process foundation; no new disclosure rule independent of the Simmons predicates is adopted.
- **Skipper v. South Carolina, 476 U.S. 1 (1986):** Its protection for rebutting a State future-danger case supports accurate parole information; its distinct mitigation protection remains intact.
- **California v. Ramos, 463 U.S. 992 (1983):** Distinguished to preserve truthful release-related information without treating hypothetical clemency as ordinary parole or requiring a guarantee against future legal change.
- **Ramdass v. Angelone, 530 U.S. 156 (2000):** Its attention to actual and conditional legal consequences is applied. Its separate habeas requirements and unresolved factual predicates do not control this direct-review remedy or establish error effect here.
- **Chapman v. California, 386 U.S. 18 (1967):** Applied without changing the State's burden to establish beyond a reasonable doubt that a constitutional error did not contribute to the death verdict.

Effective March 20, 2001, Simmons governs South Carolina's staged capital procedure and materially equivalent jury choices: an earlier conditional judicial sentence does not eliminate the information right when the jury actually chooses death or life without ordinary parole. The State-raised-danger trigger and accurate legal ineligibility premise remain necessary, and effective defense presentation or adequate instruction remains sufficient without a required script.

The combined presentation in this case fails that requirement if the danger predicate is established. Adequacy is settled for the remand. The state court must decide danger and, if present, apply Chapman's State-burden inquiry before determining whether a new capital penalty proceeding or a lawful noncapital sentence is required. This application does not overrule a precedent, impose automatic reversal or settle a broader instruction right without the danger predicate.

### Buford v. United States — 2001-03-20

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Buford_v_United_States_merits_2001-03-20.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Buford_v_United_States_merits_2001-03-20.md#precedent-treatment).

#### Deference follows the task performed

**Holding and operative rule:** Appellate courts independently review the meaning of the related-case provisions and authoritative commentary, but defer to a district court's supported, record-specific determination of functional consolidation when it depends on the procedural history and practical sentencing context. Concurrent sentences and a common hearing are relevant but not conclusive, and absence of a formal consolidation order is not itself dispositive; under that allocation, the determination here is sustained.

**Authority:** Justice Thomas's opinion is joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine directly adopt this rule and its application.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the district court performed a practical sentencing task, rather than defining a general constitutional boundary. Section 3742(e) requires due deference to application of the Guidelines to facts. Koon and Powell supply the necessary separation: legal misinterpretations and unsupported grounds remain correctable, while a supported assessment sensitive to the particular proceedings receives deference. Stinson makes consistent commentary authoritative; it does not require appellate redetermination of every application.

The four robbery convictions were concededly related. The disputed drug conviction shared a sentencing judge, hearing, presentence report and concurrent terms, and counsel agreed to simultaneous sentencing. Those facts favor consolidation and must be considered. Separate filings, prosecutors, pleas, judgments and sentences nevertheless supported the district court's contrary assessment. Familiarity with local sentencing arrangements helps identify what those proceedings actually accomplished. Neither one hearing nor the absence of a formal order supplies an automatic answer.

The increased sentencing range makes correct legal criteria important, but does not make this fact-bound application especially useful as a general appellate rule. Independent legal review, correction of unsupported inferences and the Sentencing Commission's supervisory role preserve uniformity. Ornelas and Fields require independent review of ultimate constitutional search thresholds for their distinct institutional reasons; they do not select one standard for every mixed question. No actual legal misinterpretation requiring another sentencing proceeding is identified here.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The complete substantive definition of functional consolidation and a constitutional jury-allocation claim lie outside the granted review question. The commentary's intervening-arrest exclusion remains distinct from the alternatives of offenses on the same occasion, part of a single common scheme or plan, or consolidated for trial or sentencing. No finding under another alternative is made. This decision creates no general constitutional recidivism exception and changes neither Apprendi's reserved Guidelines questions nor Almendarez-Torres's limited controlling judgment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Koon v. United States / Powell v. United States:** Applied to separate independent legal correction from deference to supported sentencing application; neither is made a blanket rule for every Guidelines question.
- **Stinson v. United States:** Applied for the authority of consistent commentary; its superior-law limits remain unchanged.
- **Ornelas v. United States; United States v. Fields / Crawley:** Distinguished by the recurring constitutional thresholds requiring independent review; their review rules remain fully effective.
- **First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan:** Its division of appellate responsibility supports attention to the task rather than a universal mixed-question label.
- **Apprendi v. New Jersey; Almendarez-Torres v. United States:** Their actual holdings and reservations are left intact; this appellate-review decision supplies no new jury or recidivism rule.

**Resulting law:** The related-case functional-consolidation application receives task-sensitive deference, with legal criteria independently reviewed and record support still required. The decision specifies this review allocation without changing the substantive commentary or constitutional search-review rules. It does not establish that simultaneous or concurrent sentencing always combines, or always fails to combine, prior convictions.

### TrafFix Devices, Inc. v. Marketing Displays, Inc. — 2001-03-20

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/TrafFix_Devices_v_Marketing_Displays_merits_2001-03-20.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/TrafFix_Devices_v_Marketing_Displays_merits_2001-03-20.md#precedent-treatment).

#### Useful features remain available to competitors

**Holding and operative rule:** An expired utility patent is strong evidence that the feature it identifies as useful is functional. When the claimed feature is essential to the article's use or purpose or affects its cost or quality, source recognition and alternative workable designs do not make it protectable trade dress; a genuinely distinct ornamental, incidental or arbitrary appearance must be evaluated on its own rather than presumed functional merely because it appears in a patented product.

**Authority:** Justice Souter's opinion, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer, directly controls the rule, application and remedy.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that trade-dress law does not continue exclusive control of useful engineering merely because buyers recognize its source. Inwood supplies the essential-use-or-purpose and cost-or-quality inquiry. Qualitex retains that inquiry while also addressing competitive disadvantage unrelated to reputation. Its competitive-necessity discussion does not require another inquiry after traditional utilitarian functionality is established. Wal-Mart separately requires source significance for product design; recognition cannot replace nonfunctionality.

The patents describe the dual springs' contribution to wind resistance and stability. That operation, supported by the record, establishes the functional character of the feature claimed here. MDI's argument concerns the overall stand, including legs, uprights and spring placement. Considering that combination does not reveal a distinct nonfunctional ornament in this record. Requiring a competitor to conceal, cover or rearrange the working springs would restrict use of the useful configuration itself. The availability of another workable arrangement therefore does not defeat the functionality ground.

The patent creates a substantial evidentiary obstacle, not a conclusive presumption covering every appearance detail. A claimant remains able to identify and support a genuinely separate nonfunctional feature. None has been established in the claim before the Court. The undisputed functional evidence is sufficient without deciding the temporal application of the 1999 burden amendment. Functionality disposes of this trade-dress theory, so secondary meaning supplies no further ground that must be reached.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The decision establishes no automatic constitutional bar to all trade dress associated with expired patents and announces no new aesthetic-functionality test. A distinct unasserted ornamental design lies outside this claim. Those questions are unnecessary or outside the granted scope, respectively; the functional dual-spring claim itself is resolved, not returned for another alternatives inquiry.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Inwood Laboratories, Inc. v. Ives Laboratories, Inc.:** Applied for the traditional essential-use-or-purpose and cost-or-quality rule.
- **Qualitex Co. v. Jacobson Products Co.:** Applied and clarified: its competitive-disadvantage discussion complements, rather than displaces, the traditional utilitarian bar. No additional competitive-necessity showing is required once that bar is met.
- **Wal-Mart Stores, Inc. v. Samara Brothers, Inc.:** Applied to keep source significance for product design separate from nonfunctionality; its actual distinctiveness rule remains unchanged.
- **Two Pesos, Inc. v. Taco Cabana, Inc.:** Its protection of otherwise qualifying trade dress does not remove the independent functionality requirement; its existing scope remains intact.

**Resulting law:** A utility patent supplies strong, feature-specific evidence of functionality. Proven useful function independently defeats trade-dress protection despite source recognition or available alternative designs. The rule leaves protection of genuinely distinct nonfunctional appearance to its own requirements and leaves the 1999 amendment's temporal reach undecided.

### Circuit City Stores, Inc. v. Adams — 2001-03-21

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Circuit_City_Stores_v_Adams_merits_2001-03-21.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Circuit_City_Stores_v_Adams_merits_2001-03-21.md#precedent-treatment).

#### Employment contracts fall within the section 1 exclusion

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1 of the Federal Arbitration Act excludes contracts of employment of workers within the commerce reach asserted for the Act; its residual employment category is not confined to transportation occupations. Adams's retail employment agreement is excluded, so the FAA supplies no authority to compel arbitration of his claims or to enjoin his state action.

**Authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's opinion is joined in full by Justices Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Those five directly adopt the statutory construction, its general employment-contract reach, its application and the affirmance.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the employment exclusion must be read with the coverage provision in the same enactment. Section 1 identifies employment contracts of seamen and railroad employees and then adds other classes of workers engaged in interstate or foreign commerce. The shared category can be workers in commerce. The residual words do not expressly restrict the exclusion to transporting persons or goods.

The employer's associated-words argument is substantial, but the inference drawn from a statutory list must remain consistent with the text's contemporary commercial meaning and setting. The named occupations placed important federal employment arrangements beyond doubt; the residual language protects the wider employment category. Reading section 2 to follow the full reach of commerce authority while confining the accompanying labor exclusion to occupations most clearly within federal authority in 1925 would disconnect the provisions. The contemporaneous labor setting confirms the broader boundary where the competing linguistic readings leave uncertainty. Concern about employee choice supports that reading but cannot itself create an exemption.

Allied-Bruce establishes section 2's actual-commerce reach and preserves its operation in state and federal court, but expressly leaves section 1 unresolved. Gilmer concerns statutory claims under a securities-registration agreement and reserves the employment-contract question. Neither supplies a contrary controlling answer. Ordinary assent and revocation principles under First Options and Casarotto govern when their predicates are present; they cannot substitute for the threshold category Congress excluded.

Adams was employed as a retail sales counselor under the employment application containing this arbitration clause. His agreement therefore falls within the exclusion without a finding that its particular procedure denied an effective remedy. The Ninth Circuit correctly removed the federal compulsion and injunction order. No existing Supreme Court holding is overruled.

**Limits and matters not reached:** Otherwise covered commercial agreements remain subject to section 2. An independent state-law basis for arbitration, discrimination liability, and classification of an independent contractor lie outside this employee's presented FAA exclusion question. The Court decides no ordinary contract defense or constitutional jury-waiver question because the statutory exclusion disposes of the requested FAA relief. The state proceeding remains subject to independently applicable law and properly preserved defenses.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson:** Preserved and distinguished. Its actual-commerce construction and section 2 state-court rule continue for otherwise covered agreements; its express employment reservation is resolved here under section 1.
- **Gilmer v. Interstate/Johnson Lane Corp.:** Distinguished because the agreement there arose from securities registration. Arbitration of statutory employment claims through such an agreement does not settle the separate employment-contract exclusion.
- **First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan:** Preserved for antecedent assent, actual delegation and review in its proper setting. It does not enlarge the FAA's statutory coverage, and no new delegation or award-review rule is adopted.
- **Doctor's Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto:** Preserved for genuinely general contract defenses and the rule against arbitration-specific disabilities in covered agreements. Those enforcement rules do not replace section 1's antecedent exclusion.
- **Green Tree Financial Corp.-Alabama v. Randolph:** Its finality, statutory-access and record-specific cost-development holdings remain effective. They supply neither a section 1 answer nor a requirement that this excluded employee first prove prohibitive arbitral costs.
- **Eastern Associated Coal Corp. v. United Mine Workers of America:** Its section 301 labor-award and public-policy analysis remains in its distinct statutory setting; it supplies no contrary construction of FAA section 1.

**Resulting law:** Section 1's previously unresolved employment-contract category now excludes contracts of employment of workers within the commerce reach asserted for the FAA, including retail employment. The Act therefore cannot supply compulsion or an injunction for this excluded agreement. Its section 2 rules remain operative for otherwise covered commercial agreements. The exclusion does not declare employment arbitration agreements categorically unenforceable under independent state law, decide the underlying claims, or impose a costs inquiry as a condition of exclusion.

### Egelhoff v. Egelhoff — 2001-03-21

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Egelhoff_v_Egelhoff_merits_2001-03-21.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Egelhoff_v_Egelhoff_merits_2001-03-21.md#precedent-treatment).

#### Automatic substitution of the beneficiary is preempted for both benefits

**Holding and operative rule:** ERISA section 514 displaces this state divorce-revocation rule to the extent it automatically substitutes the recipient of covered benefits for the plan's operative beneficiary designation. That rule defeats both identified revocation-based claims, including recovery of the already paid insurance from its recipient; the payment does not create a legally independent source of title, and pension anti-alienation is not the ground for the insurance holding.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's opinion is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas. Seven Justices directly adopt the rule, its application to both benefit demands, its limits and the confined reversal/remand.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that choosing the beneficiary is a central act of plan administration. Section 514, read with the plan-document duties, prevents the State from replacing the operative designation through this automatic divorce rule. Its application to nonplan property does not remove the instruction it gives when applied to covered benefits. An opt-out still requires affirmative plan drafting and attention to differing state rules to preserve the otherwise operative designation.

The children's strongest answer is that the statute supplies background validity law where the plans leave a gap. The investment plan's invalid-designation fallback makes that question real, but does not authorize every state rule declaring a designation invalid. The actual documents establish no continuing-marriage condition or adoption of this revocation rule. Insurance-plan silence likewise does not establish such adoption. The challenged rule changes entitlement because of a subsequent divorce, rather than identifying a demonstrated defect in execution, capacity or expression of the designation.

Travelers, Dillingham and De Buono distinguish ordinary costs and background effects from direct benefit or administrative commands. UNUM preserves that distinction even when an administrative reassignment uses ordinary-law terminology. The statute's defeasibility, notice protections and permission to withhold payment limit liability but do not eliminate its substitute-beneficiary operation. The result rests on that direct conflict, not disfavor toward family law or conjectured administrative inconvenience.

The insurance payment changes possession, not the legal source of the children's asserted entitlement. Their claim still depends on statutory revocation rather than an independent later promise or transaction. Boggs directs attention to that source, but its pension anti-alienation rule cannot be transferred to welfare insurance. Section 514 independently supplies the present insurance result. Unknown pension-payment status therefore does not prevent resolution of either legal claim.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The decision preempts these revocation-based demands, not every state rule governing capacity, execution, interpretation, marriage, inheritance or distributed money. Unlawful-killing disqualifications, simultaneous death, actual fraud, a valid domestic-relations order and independently arising postdistribution obligations lie outside the presented claim. No alternative beneficiary, actual redesignation, applicable exception, federal survivor entitlement, notice liability, benefit waiver or award is found. Ordinary implementation must follow the actual documents and governing federal protections, with properly preserved independent grounds retained.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Travelers; Dillingham; De Buono:** Their distinction between ordinary background or economic effects and direct benefit or administrative commands is applied, not displaced. General applicability is no automatic exemption, and family-law subject matter is no automatic ground for preemption.
- **UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward:** Its concrete administrative-connection rule is applied to beneficiary substitution. Ordinary interpretive principles are not categorically preempted, just as UNUM did not establish a universal federal agency code.
- **Boggs v. Boggs:** Its attention to the asserted source of benefit entitlement is applied; its pension and survivor holdings retain their actual scope. The present paid-insurance conclusion rests independently on section 514 and does not extend pension anti-alienation to welfare insurance.
- **Mackey v. Lanier Collection Agency & Service, Inc.:** The distinction between pension restrictions and welfare-benefit treatment remains effective. Ordinary recipient obligations are not categorically displaced merely because property once came from a plan.

**Resulting law:** Automatic divorce revocation cannot substitute the beneficiary of these covered pension or life-insurance benefits, including through a recipient recovery resting on that same statutory source. The decision adds this concrete application of section 514 without expanding remote-effects preemption or insulating all distributed assets. The actual plan documents, applicable federal protections and genuinely independent preserved grounds govern implementation; payment status does not change the source of the claims decided here.

### Ferguson v. City of Charleston — 2001-03-21

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Ferguson_v_City_of_Charleston_merits_2001-03-21.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Ferguson_v_City_of_Charleston_merits_2001-03-21.md#precedent-treatment).

#### The coordinated prosecution program lacks an adequate special-needs justification

**Holding and operative rule:** Assuming legally insufficient consent, the public hospital's systematic collection and testing of patient urine under the arranged disclosure, evidence-preservation and arrest program is not justified by the special-needs exception. Genuine clinical work and a treatment objective do not themselves supply an independently adequate justification for this program's designed prosecution operation; the special-needs affirmance must be reversed, with the preserved consent issue and remaining claim-specific consequences returned for ordinary adjudication.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's opinion is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. All seven directly adopt this confined rule, its application and the reversal/remand. No general immediate-versus-ultimate-purpose rule is adopted.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the actual operation must have an adequate justification for dispensing with ordinary Fourth Amendment protection. Chandler requires a substantial operational need, and Edmond prevents an ordinary narcotics-evidence program from obtaining an exception merely through additional legitimate functions. Vernonia recognizes urine collection and analysis as searches despite participation in a public program; its confined athletic safety arrangements and nonpolice use do not authorize the present operation.

The hospital began clinical screening and referrals before police cooperation. Its concern for maternal and infant health was genuine, and the district court's health-goal finding is not erased. But the later policy prescribed chain of custody, police disclosure and arrest criteria. Four plaintiffs were arrested without a treatment alternative; later arrests followed noncompliance or another positive. Those designed means require justification at their actual scope. Beneficial ends alone cannot distinguish this evidence program from ordinary prosecution intended to prevent harm.

Griffin establishes that police cooperation and later criminal use do not invariably defeat special needs. It sustained a probation search under a valid supervisory regulation, conditional liberty and the institutional need for prompt action on reasonable grounds. Ordinary patients here are not subject to that lawful supervisory relationship, and the record establishes no independently adequate medical justification for the challenged penal implementation. This distinguishes Griffin without requiring custody, emergency, quantified treatment failure or exhaustion of alternatives in every special-needs case. Skinner and Von Raab likewise rest on their operative protective arrangements.

The assumed absence of adequate consent resolves only the exception question. The defense jury verdict was not reviewed for evidentiary sufficiency on appeal. The Court therefore returns that preserved challenge rather than overturning the verdict on unreviewed facts. Medical attendance alone is no waiver, but neither a universal three-permission rule nor a final patient-specific consent finding follows.

**Limits and matters not reached:** Consent to medical care and the scope of consent to the challenged evidence operation require distinct, fact-sensitive analysis. The trial's collection/testing/disclosure instruction is not adopted as a universal constitutional formula, and disclosure alone is not held to be a separate search. The preserved consent challenge and live individual responsibility, municipal attribution, immunity, causation, injury and relief questions remain for their proper adjudication. Private-hospital conduct, independent mandatory-reporting duties, genuine emergent treatment, materially different authorized clinical programs and suppression in separate criminal prosecutions lie outside this case. No damages, universal testing injunction, automatic new trial or new evidentiary hearing is ordered.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Chandler v. Miller:** Applied to require an actual adequate need tied to the program's operation; no mandatory catastrophe, quantitative efficacy, emergency or universal exhaustion condition is added.
- **City of Indianapolis v. Edmond:** Applied for the need for an independently lawful justification for ordinary narcotics-evidence gathering. The present bodily-testing application does not turn every additional enforcement purpose into a categorical disqualification.
- **Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton:** Its urine-search and no-waiver rules are applied. Its particular school-athletic safety, privacy and restricted-use justification is distinguished; it supplies no ordinary police-testing authority over patients.
- **Griffin v. Wisconsin:** Preserved as a mixed supervision/enforcement decision. Its actual probation regulation, conditional liberty and institutional justification distinguish this ordinary-patient program; police cooperation or later prosecution alone is not universally disqualifying.
- **Skinner v. Railway Labor Executives' Association; National Treasury Employees Union v. Von Raab:** Their contextual safety and public-service justifications remain effective at their actual scope and do not authorize this coordinated patient-evidence operation.

**Resulting law:** The special-needs exception does not sustain this public-hospital program's planned collection, testing and prosecution operation merely because real medical work and beneficial health objectives coexist with it. An independently sufficient justification must address the actual intrusion and use; no categorical rule against all mixed purposes is adopted. Valid consent and other genuinely applicable exceptions retain their separate requirements, and the decision itself establishes no final patient-specific liability or remedy.

### Gibbs v. Babbitt — 2001-03-21

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Gibbs_v_Babbitt_merits_2001-03-21.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Gibbs_v_Babbitt_merits_2001-03-21.md#precedent-treatment).

#### Part I. The enacted taking class and the required connection

**Holding and operative rule:** The regulated class is the taking of protected wildlife without a commercial element. A landowner's commercial motive, the wolves' past interstate transportation, projected tourism, scientific value and potential trade do not, alone or by accumulation, establish the constitutionally required interstate connection; aggregation measures effects after an eligible connection is identified and cannot supply the missing connection.

**Authority:** Part I of Justice O'Connor's opinion has five direct joins: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Ginsburg and Breyer accept the governing framework for the remand but do not join this part's restrictive connection premise. The five direct joins control without aggregation of separate rationales.

**Controlling explanation:** Lopez requires identification of the enacted class and its actual interstate mechanism before aggregate magnitude can establish power. Home Builders applies that requirement to wildlife taking; an owner's commercial project does not rewrite the class Congress regulated. Morrison preserves the requirement despite extensive evidence of downstream economic consequences.

The present rule prohibits taking experimental red wolves on private land without making commercial activity an element. Mann's concern for cattle therefore does not transform the regulated class into cattle ranching. Actual refuge visitors and scientific work are relevant evidence, but their economic consequences do not by themselves establish the necessary relationship between this taking class and interstate commerce. Nor does the wolves' earlier transport establish that every later local taking is itself interstate commerce. The government must establish an eligible mechanism under the governing framework rather than combine several incomplete connections. The Court does not insert a new commercial element into the regulation or adopt a categorical rule immunizing all noneconomic local conduct.

#### Part II. Examine the existing record under both complete routes

**Holding and operative rule:** The judgment sustaining these private-land applications must be vacated for a confined examination of the existing legislative and adjudicative record. The government must identify and materially support either a valid federal interstate regime that exclusion of this taking class would evade or materially impair, or a concrete substantial cross-border effect that affected States cannot reasonably confine or internalize through ordinary territorial authority, with a reasonable fit between the prohibition and the effect; if neither complete route is supported, appropriate party- and application-specific declaratory or injunctive relief must follow.

**Authority:** Part II of Justice O'Connor's opinion and the vacatur/remand have five direct joins: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer. Ginsburg and Breyer affirmatively join this complete inquiry and remedy under the controlling connection framework, while preserving their proposed broader ground for affirmance. Those five directly adopt the complete inquiry and remedy; no broader rationale commands a majority.

**Controlling explanation:** Home Builders supplies both routes and the government's obligation to support the mechanism it invokes. Courts independently decide constitutional eligibility while allowing supported legislative predictions and reasonable regulatory design within the power. An actual interstate regime and its anti-evasion or material-impairment relationship must be identified; a comprehensive conservation program and federal refuge ownership do not alone establish that relationship. The externality route requires a supported substantial effect across state boundaries, a real territorial-control limit, and fit. State policy disagreement, national interest and ordinary movement from a refuge to nearby private land are insufficient by themselves.

The existing record reports actual interstate refuge visitors and a population occupying both refuge and private land. Those facts warrant examination under the correct mechanisms; disputes over revenue forecasts do not erase the visitors. Conversely, no interstate wolf migration, failed state cooperation, economic market or causal necessity may be invented. Agency accounts, program records, testimony and reasonable circumstantial inference may supply support without a mandatory statistical study, fixed revenue threshold or proof that every animal crosses a state line.

Morrison denies a general entitlement to repeated opportunities after an inadequate commerce theory. This remand instead identifies evidence already present that the lower courts did not evaluate under Home Builders. It permits no indefinite search for new science or replacement rationales. Solid Waste decided statutory authority, and the separate ecological position there involved a different mobile interstate bird population; neither supplies missing wolf facts. The court on remand must determine whether the existing support completes either route. If not, confined relief is required.

**Limits and remaining work:** The governing legal routes are settled; the remaining task is to evaluate the existing record under them, not to discover an additional legal test. Neither mechanism is found proved in this decision. The Property Clause, treaty theory and any takings-compensation claim lie outside the granted Commerce question. Mann's earlier conviction is not reviewed. Facts needed for a particular future enforcement event are absent and are not found here. Relief remains tied to the parties and challenged private-land applications. The remand itself authorizes no taking contrary to otherwise applicable law, and orders neither damages nor general invalidation of the ESA.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **United States v. Lopez:** Applied and retained for the enacted-class, eligible-connection, aggregation and fit sequence. Remote movement and generalized economic consequences do not create the required connection; no categorical immunity for noneconomic conduct is added.
- **National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt:** Applied in full at each proposition's controlling scope. Its necessary-regime and independent-externality routes, material support, territorial-control limit, predictive latitude and confined existing-record remedy govern this challenge.
- **United States v. Morrison:** Applied and reaffirmed. Its rejection of downstream magnitude as a substitute for connection remains intact, as does its refusal to give every failed constitutional theory another evidentiary opportunity. The identified existing-record inquiry here creates no such general right.
- **Solid Waste Agency of Northern Cook County v. U.S. Army Corps of Engineers:** Its statutory holding is distinguished from the constitutional question here. It leaves the Commerce framework unchanged; the separate bird-population analysis establishes no red-wolf fact or controlling constitutional rule.
- **Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon:** Its statutory taking and causation rules remain unchanged. This Commerce decision neither creates a new taking exception nor awards a permit, and does not substitute habitat-injury doctrine for proof of a constitutional mechanism.

**Resulting law:** The existing Home Builders framework governs the challenged noncommercial private-land taking class. The decision neither adopts the proposed broader substantial-class-effects rule nor replaces the independent-externality route with an economic-conduct limitation. Existing evidence must materially support one complete mechanism and its fit; if it does not, confined relief is required. No current finding validates or categorically invalidates every application of the experimental-population rule.

### Texas v. Cobb — 2001-04-02

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Texas_v_Cobb_merits_2001-04-02.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Texas_v_Cobb_merits_2001-04-02.md#precedent-treatment).

**Common authority:** Justice Breyer's opinion is joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg. All five directly adopt both holdings and their operative limits.

#### 1. Representation protects interrogation about an inseparable part of the charged occurrence

**Holding and operative rule:** Once prosecution and representation begin, the Sixth Amendment counsel protection extends to questioning about an additional offense so integrated with the charged occurrence that the questioning necessarily reconstructs the same represented criminal episode. Different statutory elements do not alone remove that protection; shared participants, a similar method or a general criminal association, without that inseparable connection, do not establish it.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the State cannot divide this represented occurrence into separate charging labels and then treat interrogation of the same event as outside counsel's protection. McNeil makes the right offense-specific and leaves genuinely unrelated investigations open. It does not settle how closely integrated additional charges should be classified. Moulton protects the accused against deliberate circumvention of counsel while another offense is investigated. Today's rule develops that protection for an additional prosecution inseparable from the represented episode; Moulton did not already decide this precise boundary.

Blockburger and Brown identify legally identical and greater or lesser included offenses for double jeopardy. Their elements inquiry serves the separate task of determining legislatively distinct crimes. It is insufficient as the exclusive boundary of assistance in defending an actual prosecution. The State's proposed rule would allow the order in which integrated charges are filed to separate the counsel safeguard from the investigation it protects. A prohibition on use only in the initially charged case would leave that practical circumvention intact.

Cobb had been indicted for the burglary and was represented by appointed counsel. The murders occurred during that burglary, and the confession reconstructed the same occurrence. The capital count charged multiple murders in one criminal transaction; it was not a burglary-felony-murder count. The statutory differences therefore are real, but do not answer the counsel question. Earlier requests for counsel's permission to interview Cobb confirm that the relationship was apparent; those permissions did not authorize the later interrogation. The intimate connection of this investigation supplies an objective boundary without making every represented person immune from questioning about separate crimes.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The rule does not extend to every factually associated crime, rest on an officer's unexpressed motive, or bar speaking to represented persons as witnesses. Ordinary attachment requirements remain. Unrelated crimes and a materially different investigative connection are outside this holding.

#### 2. A custodial waiver does not replace the applicable attached-right waiver protection

**Holding and operative rule:** A valid Miranda waiver does not, by itself, authorize police-initiated deliberate elicitation about the protected episode after the accused has asserted the attached counsel right within Michigan v. Jackson. On the state court's established assertion and prejudice determinations, this confession must be excluded and its new-trial judgment stands.

**Controlling explanation:** Miranda protects a suspect during custodial interrogation. The Sixth Amendment separately protects assistance after prosecution begins. McNeil preserves that distinction; satisfying one safeguard does not establish a waiver under the other. Jackson governs police-initiated questioning after assertion of the attached right. The state court treated Cobb's acceptance of appointed counsel as that assertion and rejected unilateral waiver in the later encounter. The State cannot evade that protection merely by calling the closely integrated murders a different offense.

Prior counsel permission related to earlier interviews, and no permission for this interrogation is established. Nor is this a defendant-initiated encounter. Applying the existing Jackson protection answers the State's alternative custodial-waiver contention without redefining every form of Sixth Amendment waiver. The state court found the confession's admission prejudicial. The Court does not replace that determination with a new factual assessment or presume that independent evidence cannot support a lawful retrial.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The Court does not hold that appointment universally invokes Fifth Amendment counsel protection, overrule Jackson, or decide a defendant-initiated encounter or a genuinely valid Sixth Amendment waiver. No independent coercion claim is adjudicated. Davis's requirement to clarify a reasonable possible present request remains effective in its own custodial setting; no such new request is found here.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **McNeil v. Wisconsin:** Preserved for offense specificity and the distinction between Fifth and Sixth Amendment protections; its unresolved offense boundary now includes the inseparable represented occurrence described here, while unrelated investigations remain outside attachment.
- **Maine v. Moulton:** Extended from protection against circumvention in the charged prosecution to an additional prosecution inseparable from that represented occurrence. The original charged-case protection remains effective.
- **Brewer v. Williams:** Preserved for the protection against deliberate elicitation after attachment; it supplies no finding of a new waiver here.
- **Blockburger v. United States:** Retained as an elements test in its double-jeopardy setting, but rejected as the exclusive definition of counsel scope.
- **Brown v. Ohio:** Its greater and lesser included offense treatment remains intact; that legal identity does not exhaust the represented-episode protection.
- **Michigan v. Jackson:** Applied to the state court's established assertion and police-initiated encounter; a valid custodial waiver does not displace its independent protection.
- **Miranda v. Arizona:** Preserved as an independent custodial safeguard. The valid waiver here does not settle the attached Sixth Amendment question.
- **Davis v. United States:** Preserved, including mandatory clarification of a reasonable possible present request; no new request or clarification violation is found.
- **Dickerson v. United States:** Preserved for Miranda's constitutional status; it does not merge the distinct counsel protections or establish a Sixth Amendment waiver.

**Resulting law:** The offense-specific counsel right now includes interrogation necessarily reconstructing an inseparable part of the represented criminal occurrence, although the additional offense has different elements. The rule does not cover every factual association or unrelated offense. Existing attachment, assertion and waiver requirements remain distinct, and Miranda compliance alone does not displace Jackson. The decision supplies no general immunity from investigation and leaves double-jeopardy elements doctrine unchanged.

### Wallis v. Spencer — 2001-04-02

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Wallis_v_Spencer_merits_2001-04-02.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Wallis_v_Spencer_merits_2001-04-02.md#precedent-treatment).

#### I. Emergency removal requires an immediate danger and a response confined to it

**Holding and operative rule:** Unilateral, nonconsensual removal of children from parental custody without prior judicial authorization requires specific, articulable information giving objectively reasonable grounds to believe that imminent serious bodily harm requires action before authorization can reasonably be obtained; the removal's scope and duration must be reasonably directed to that danger. Genuine disputes about the information, urgency and scope here prevent summary judgment for the City on the preserved removal claim, without establishing a constitutional violation or municipal liability.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion is joined throughout by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Stone-Zsela joins Parts I, II.A and III, and concurs only in the judgment on Parts II.B and IV. All nine directly adopt this removal holding and support the complete partial affirmance, partial vacatur and remand. The removal rule states necessary protections for the intervention presented, not an exhaustive test for every parental-right claim; no combination of separate rationales supplies its authority.

**Controlling explanation:** The children's Fourth Amendment protection against unreasonable seizure and the parents' Fourteenth Amendment family interests are distinct. Troxel protects parental direction against displacement by ordinary governmental preference but supplies no complete custody test. McArthur requires attention to actual need, execution, duration and available judicial authorization; its limited residential restraint does not itself authorize this greater bodily and familial intrusion.

The reported threat to kill Jessie at the equinox was grave. Transmission through a therapist and protective-services personnel could support prompt protective action without certainty of abuse. Neither the report's later disproof nor the aunt's psychiatric treatment alone defeats that possibility. But the disclosed reliability problems, earlier unfounded report, disputed therapist communications, investigation actually performed and children's observed condition cannot be resolved against the family on summary judgment. No judge issued a removal order. Whether CPS reported one or requested removal, and what the officers knew, remain disputed.

The allegation did not implicate Becky or threaten Lauren. Those differences and practical protective alternatives bear on the intrusion's scope; they do not establish that another placement was actually available. A date-specific threat cannot by itself justify indefinite separation. Permission to enter the home does not establish consent to removal or examinations.

Courts determine the governing law and may decide sufficiency on undisputed facts. The Court does not assign every reasonable-cause question to a jury, impose a fixed corroboration category, require exhaustion of every imaginable alternative, or determine credibility here. Nor does it establish a universal unfitness prerequisite, strict-scrutiny framework or equivalence of the two Amendments. The legal consequence of mistaken reliance on a reported order remains unresolved; the described appellate omission establishes no forfeiture at every stage.

**Precedent treatment:** Troxel's protected parental direction is applied at its controlling scope; its separate broader frameworks remain noncontrolling. McArthur's attention to actual need, scope and duration is applied without transferring its particular residential authorization to child removal.

#### II.A. Municipal responsibility requires the City's own actionable policy, fault and causal connection

**Holding and operative rule:** A section 1983 municipal claim requires a federal deprivation caused by the City's own attributable policy or legally effective custom and the fault required by the theory actually presented; a training, supervision or safeguards omission requires deliberate indifference, supported notice and an identified deficiency closely related to and actually causing the injury. The testimony connecting an asserted settled removal practice to this intervention defeats the categorical conclusion that no policy evidence exists; the remaining elements are unproved.

**Authority:** The same nine Justices directly join this holding and its limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Collins separates a federal deprivation from municipal responsibility. Monell rejects employer-based federal liability. Carter applies Canton to deliberate municipal omissions: notice may arise from an established pattern or a sufficiently obvious need to address a clear constitutional duty in known recurring circumstances. Prior identical incidents are not invariably required. Negligence, employee error and hindsight that further training might have helped do not suffice.

The City correctly argues that an officer's mistake or outside misinformation alone cannot establish its responsibility. Here, however, a supervisor and the designated knowledgeable officer described a settled practice of accepting reported orders without verification. Evidence connects reliance on the supposed order to removal and the absence of further investigation. That permits the policy claim to proceed; it does not establish that the practice was unconstitutional, that every witness possessed final policymaking authority, or that the City caused every later injury.

An affirmative policy and an omission remain different theories. The latter requires proof of deliberate indifference, notice, the particular deficiency and actual causation; the duration of a practice alone proves none of those elements. Final authority depends on the legally effective allocation of power for the challenged function, not operational discretion alone. Interagency cooperation does not make county personnel or a physician City employees. No universal paper-order inspection requirement or prohibition of telephone communication follows.

Ordinary pleading and proof controls continue, without a special requirement to plead inaccessible internal operations in heightened detail.

**Precedent treatment:** Collins and Monell preserve separate deprivation and attribution requirements. Carter and Canton govern omission fault, notice and causation; McMillian governs function-specific final authority. Leatherman preserves ordinary municipal pleading.

#### II.B. The City cannot borrow personal or state immunity against section 1983

**Holding and operative rule:** The City cannot defeat its own federal municipal liability by borrowing an individual's personal qualified or absolute immunity or by invoking state immunity against section 1983. Rejection of those categorical defenses is affirmed without deciding any absent individual's immunity or liability.

**Authority:** Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this ground. Stone-Zsela concurs in this disposition without adding grounds.

**Controlling explanation:** Carter separates municipal responsibility from the personal immunity an officer may possess. A defense protecting an individual's performance of a function does not exempt the City's own actionable policy from federal law. State immunity likewise does not displace the federal municipal claim.

The City is the only remaining defendant in this appeal. Its inability to borrow these defenses does not adjudicate an officer's immunity or restore a personal claim. Denius preserves particularized objective notice from the relevant law when the individual acted; an identical earlier case is unnecessary, but Ferguson in 2001 cannot supply September 1991 notice. The Court resolves only the City's categorical defenses, leaving the constitutional deprivation, institutional fault, causation and injury requirements intact.

**Precedent treatment:** Carter's separation of municipal responsibility and personal immunity is applied. Denius's conduct-date notice rule remains intact and supplies no retrospective notice or municipal immunity.

#### III. The independent examination theory requires a threshold determination, while consequential injury remains possible

**Holding and operative rule:** The independent City examination-policy adjudication and its associated constitutional rules are vacated insofar as they conclusively govern the remand; the lower court must first determine that theory's actual presentation and scope under ordinary procedural rules before adjudicating its independent merits. Examination injuries caused by a proved removal-policy violation do not invariably require a second examination policy, but the underlying wrong, actual causal connection, injury and recoverable measure must still be established separately for each claimed consequence.

**Authority:** All nine Justices directly join this threshold and causation holding. No independent examination merits rule is decided. Stone-Zsela's concurrence states his own conditional bodily-integrity rule and receives no other join.

**Controlling explanation:** The examinations were substantial bodily intrusions. The district court addressed them, the report identified the City as requesting agency, and testimony concerned the detective and hospital contract. Both questions were granted. These circumstances support arguments about presentation and attribution; they do not resolve the specific objection that an independent City examination-policy claim was never properly presented. Review does not amend a complaint. Neither a lower dissent nor the absence of original filings here establishes forfeiture.

The proper course is to determine that threshold without conclusively imposing the lower court's independent examination, notice, opportunity-to-object or parental-presence rules. Even its presence rule permitted exclusion for a valid reason with nearby presence instead. Today's judgment finds neither preservation nor waiver and neither constitutional nor unconstitutional examinations.

Ferguson requires an adequate justification for the particular prosecution operation it addressed, while preserving genuinely different clinical functions and consent issues. It supplies no universal mixed-purpose prohibition or three-permission formula. Denius's public-employment information ruling supplies no bodily-examination protocol. The present record establishes no urgent medical need, imminent evidence loss, valid substitute consent or waiver; protective custody alone cannot establish those missing predicates. The Court nonetheless leaves the independent merits question for a properly presented claim. The 1998 California examination enactment does not authorize the 1991 acts.

The removal claim's possible consequential-injury route remains distinct, as the lower opinion's note 15 recognized. Failure of a separate examination-policy theory would not automatically extinguish that route; sequence alone does not prove it. Initial removal, prehearing examinations and posthearing separation require their own causal analysis. A later medical report and judicial intervention neither automatically sever nor establish City responsibility for the entire two-and-one-half-month separation. Ordinary proceedings supply this inquiry, not a mandated new evidentiary hearing or permission to manufacture a new claim.

**Precedent treatment:** Ferguson and Denius are preserved within their distinct operational and informational scopes; neither decides this unadjudicated independent claim. Monell and Carter continue to require an actual municipal causal connection even when a second policy is unnecessary to recover consequential injury.

#### IV. The state claims return under their own liability and immunity rules

**Holding and operative rule:** The surviving California claims return within their existing scope for ordinary application of state liability and immunity law; federal restrictions on municipal respondeat-superior liability and borrowed personal immunity do not replace that separate state inquiry. Disputed CPS enlistment and the character of the challenged acts prevent disposition here under Government Code section 820.2, while the lower court's rejection of the reply-only section 821.6 theory is not reopened.

**Authority:** Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this limited state-law ground. Stone-Zsela concurs in the state-law remand without adding grounds; no independent constitutional examination holding is supplied through it.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 815.2(a)'s employee-based state liability and section 815.2(b)'s derivative immunity operate under their own terms. Section 820.2 protects an act or omission resulting from vested discretion even if that discretion is abused. Newton and Alicia T., as applied below, protect child-abuse investigation and public employees reasonably enlisted to assist, including the decision to investigate and actions necessary for a meaningful investigation. Protection does not extend to nondiscretionary acts or at least some intentional torts, including battery and false imprisonment under Newton. Whether CPS enlisted these officers, whether particular conduct involved discretion, and which pleaded tort claims fall outside protection remain unresolved. A claimed mandatory policy does not become discretionary merely because the setting involves child protection.

The lower court left the tort-specific limits for initial determination below. This Court neither extends that immunity nor decides battery, abduction, assault or intentional-infliction liability. Its rejection of the section 821.6 contention rested on reply-only presentation and the City's disclaimer of participation in the dependency prosecution; neither is reopened. Preserved state examination issues remain governed by their actual scope and state law, without importing the vacated independent federal examination rules.

**Precedent treatment:** Newton v. County of Napa and Alicia T. v. County of Los Angeles retain their stated state-law roles in the remand; neither becomes a new federal immunity rule. Monell's federal rejection of respondeat superior does not displace the distinct California statutory route.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Troxel v. Granville, 530 U.S. 57:** Applies protected parental direction against displacement by ordinary governmental preference. No complete custody framework, unfitness prerequisite, general harm formula or scrutiny level is imported from its separate positions.
- **Illinois v. McArthur, decided February 20, 2001:** Applies attention to actual justification, scope, duration and judicial authorization; its confined residential restraint supplies no automatic authority for removing children or examining their bodies.
- **Collins v. City of Harker Heights, 503 U.S. 115:** Applies the distinction between an underlying federal deprivation and municipal responsibility; institutional fault cannot substitute for a constitutional injury.
- **Monell v. Department of Social Services, 436 U.S. 658:** Applies the municipality's own policy or legally effective custom and causal responsibility; section 1983 supplies no respondeat-superior liability.
- **Carter v. City of Philadelphia, decided March 13, 2000:** Applies policy, omission fault, notice, identified deficiency and causation separately; a municipality cannot borrow personal immunity, and institutional cooperation alone creates no employment relationship.
- **City of Canton v. Harris, 489 U.S. 378:** Preserves deliberate indifference and actual causation for training or supervision omissions; neither negligence nor hindsight suffices, and identical prior incidents are not universally necessary.
