- **McMillian v. Monroe County, decided June 2, 1997:** Preserves the inquiry into legally effective final authority for the challenged function; operational discretion alone does not establish it.
- **Leatherman v. Tarrant County Narcotics Intelligence and Coordination Unit, 507 U.S. 163:** Preserves ordinary municipal pleading without heightened internal-policy particulars; ordinary discovery and proof requirements remain.
- **Denius v. Dunlap, decided January 17, 2001:** Preserves particularized conduct-date personal-immunity notice and the distinction between justified intimate-information acquisition and an unrestricted employment demand. Neither supplies a bodily-examination protocol or municipal immunity.
- **Ferguson v. City of Charleston, decided March 21, 2001:** Preserves the confined actual-operation ruling and unresolved consent inquiry; it establishes no categorical mixed-purpose prohibition, universal three-permission rule or independent holding on these examinations.
- **Newton v. County of Napa, 266 Cal. Rptr. 682; Alicia T. v. County of Los Angeles, 271 Cal. Rptr. 513:** Retain their state-law treatment of discretionary child-protection investigation and enlisted assistance, with the nondiscretionary and tort-specific limits reserved below. Neither alters federal municipal liability.

**Resulting law:** Unilateral emergency removal must rest on particularized grounds of imminent serious bodily harm requiring action before judicial authorization can reasonably be obtained, with scope and duration fitted to that danger. This necessary protection does not establish a comprehensive family-law scrutiny standard. The City's own actionable policy or omission, fault and actual causal responsibility remain separate from both a constitutional deprivation and an individual's immunity.

No Supreme Court merits rule governs the independent examination claim from this disposition. Its presentation must be determined before adjudication. A separate examination policy is not invariably needed for examination injury actually caused by a proved removal-policy violation; neither chronology nor judicial intervention alone resolves causation. The California claims retain their separate statutory liability and immunity rules. Stone-Zsela's conditional examination standard remains a concurrence.

### Lujan v. G & G Fire Sprinklers, Inc. — 2001-04-17

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Lujan_v_G_and_G_Fire_Sprinklers_merits_2001-04-17.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Lujan_v_G_and_G_Fire_Sprinklers_merits_2001-04-17.md#precedent-treatment).

#### Effective judicial contract proceedings supply the process required for this disputed debt

**Holding and operative rule:** When a State contests a public-contract payment, an actually available judicial contract proceeding capable of determining and enforcing the claimant's entitlement can supply due process without a duplicate administrative hearing. Assuming G & G has a protected contract interest, the supported ordinary action against its prime contractors defeats the categorical no-process challenge here; an inaccessible action, a conclusive unreviewable assessment or destruction of the claim would not satisfy this rule.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion, joined throughout by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine directly adopt the rule, its explanation, limits and remedy; no aggregation of separate rationales is necessary.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an opportunity to obtain a judicial decision on this disputed contractual debt supplies the process due. G & G challenges state officials' direction to withhold payment for alleged prevailing-wage violations. American Manufacturers distinguishes an enforceable claim from an unconditional right to advance payment. Its separate private-carrier holding does not immunize the State's own command, and its protection of fair adjudication remains intact.

The relevant interest is payment allegedly owed for completed work, not money already possessed, an occupational license or an established benefit award. Good protects existing possessory control. Mathews requires process suited to the actual interest, while Logan prevents the State from destroying a protected claim through its own deficient machinery. Those principles do not require a second administrative forum where an ordinary court can decide and enforce this debt.

The statutory action by the contractor or its assignee, a person to whom it transfers the claim, against the awarding body is distinct from an ordinary contract action against the prime contractor. The first has a ninety-day period after completion and acceptance and its own exclusive-remedy provision. No assignment or timely filing is presumed. J & K Painting supports the separate ordinary route against the prime; lack of an assignment does not by itself establish that the subcontractor has no judicial remedy. California courts retain final authority to construe that state law.

G & G's strongest objection is that dependence on another party's assignment or a conclusive official assessment could make judicial relief illusory. Such a supported obstacle would matter. But no established bar forecloses the separate ordinary action on this record. Normal litigation delay does not alone destroy this debt claim; neither does the Court declare every cost or delay immaterial for all interests. The ruling addresses adequacy of process, not a requirement to exhaust state remedies before a federal civil-rights action under section 1983. Future administrative amendments supply no part of today's ground.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The Court assumes the asserted property interest and decides adequate process; it establishes no universal absence of contract property and no general private-state-action rule. Wage violations, penalties, the amount owed, any actual assignment, timely filing and ultimate recovery are not decided. An actually preserved, supported obstacle to all effective adjudication must be addressed if established. The procedure taking effect July 1, 2001 does not govern this judgment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan:** Applied to distinguish a protected adjudicable claim from unconditional advance payment. Its private-carrier attribution ruling does not resolve actual state-directed withholding; its limits preserving fair adjudication, prior awards and established continuation interests remain effective.
- **Mathews v. Eldridge:** Applied through attention to the disputed contractual interest and the actual judicial forum, without a categorical requirement of duplicate administrative procedure.
- **United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property:** Distinguished because seizure of existing possessory control differs from this disputed unpaid debt; its protections remain effective.
- **Logan v. Zimmerman Brush Co.:** Preserved against destruction or effective foreclosure of a protected claim. A judicial action that cannot decide the entitlement would not provide the process on which this judgment rests.
- **J & K Painting Co. v. Bradshaw:** Its explanation of an ordinary subcontractor action supports availability independently of the statutory assignee route. It establishes neither an actual assignment nor successful recovery in this case, and California courts retain final authority over state-law construction.

**Resulting law:** Due process for this disputed public-contract debt can be supplied by meaningful ordinary judicial contract adjudication. Absence of a hearing in the withholding statute alone does not establish a constitutional violation. The remedy must actually permit determination and enforcement of entitlement; the ruling does not validate seizure of possessed property, termination of established awards, conclusive unreviewable wage assessments or foreclosure of all remedies. No general section 1983 exhaustion rule follows.

### United States v. Cleveland Indians Baseball Co. — 2001-04-17

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/United_States_v_Cleveland_Indians_Baseball_merits_2001-04-17.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/United_States_v_Cleveland_Indians_Baseball_merits_2001-04-17.md#precedent-treatment).

**Common authority:** Justice Thomas's opinion is joined throughout by Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Seven Justices directly adopt both holdings and the common regulatory explanation. Stone-Zsela and Scalia agree with the judgment and resulting payment-year applications on the distinct grounds described below; neither joins the Court's complete rationale.

#### 1. The established interpretation lawfully places these back wages in the payment-year tax account

**Holding and operative rule:** Treasury's fair and considered interpretation of the applicable rate and separate annual-base regulations requires these taxable salary awards to enter the employer's payment-year FICA and FUTA calculations. The interpretation fits the statutes and regulations and receives controlling weight under Auer; wage status alone does not settle timing, and the Court does not hold that the statutes unambiguously compel this treatment independently of the lawful administrative construction.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the actual, established interpretation governs this unresolved allocation question. The regulations distinguish remuneration paid or received in a calendar year from services performed in that year. The rate provisions select the payment-year percentage, while separate annual-base provisions identify the year's wage account. They do not expressly address backpay. Revenue Rulings 78-336 and 89-35 evidence a preexisting construction placing back wages in the payment year, rather than a position newly invented for this litigation.

Auer gives a responsible agency's fair and considered interpretation of its own regulation controlling weight unless plainly erroneous or inconsistent with that rule. Thomas Jefferson University requires the agency's actual ground, not a judicial substitute. The identified construction fits each payment and annual-base provision and remains within the statutes. Christensen prevents interpretation from amending clear regulatory text and distinguishes an advisory statutory view from an own-regulation construction; publication of a revenue ruling alone does not confer independent controlling statutory force.

Nierotko gives the Company's contrary argument real force. It classified backpay as wages and allocated it to earlier benefit periods despite paid-quarter language. But restoring a worker's service-based benefit account does not determine employer taxes under the later annual rate and base provisions. Common wage status does not require every statutory account to use identical timing. Payment-year taxation can disadvantage either the taxpayer or the Treasury. In Nierotko's benefit setting, inflexible payment-year attribution instead could only disadvantage the employee. That structural difference permits the differing allocation despite real tax anomalies; it does not eliminate them. The tax scheme permits the existing payment-based construction, while avoiding repeated reconstruction of prior payroll accounts supports its reasonableness. Administrative convenience neither creates authority nor overrides contrary text.

Gitlitz enforces an express statutory classification and sequence. It does not make this wage stipulation a concession about attribution or remove genuine interpretive room for a salary-damages substitute. The Company's preferred tax saving is not itself an enacted entitlement. The Court therefore sustains the actual regulatory construction without changing the governing deference rules or disturbing Nierotko's benefit holdings.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The applicable payment rule recognizes actual or constructive payment; no earlier constructive payment is established, and an earlier due date alone does not establish it. The separate nonqualified-deferred-compensation statutes use the later of performance of services and lapse of substantial forfeiture risk; no such plan is established here. The rule concerns these stipulated non-interest salary awards and does not decide the separate minor-cash deemed-payment category, other unpresented payment categories or individual benefit accounts. The independent binding force of revenue rulings as a general class is unnecessary and remains undecided.

#### 2. Rates and annual bases must each be applied under their 1994 provisions

**Holding and operative rule:** These 1994 payments use each tax's 1994 statutory rate and its separate 1994 annual wage-base rule; an unchanged percentage or ceiling does not permit combining the payment with another year's wage account. The operative component applications are:

| Employer-tax component | Governing 1994 treatment | Distinct consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Social Security rate, section 3111(a) | 6.2 percent | The 1986 and 1987 rate of 5.7 percent does not govern these payments. |
| Social Security annual base, section 3121(a)(1) | $60,600 | The earlier $42,000 and $43,800 bases do not carry forward with the salary losses. |
| Medicare rate, section 3111(b) | 1.45 percent | The same percentage in the earlier years does not resolve the base question. |
| Medicare wage base | No former wage ceiling | The separate Social Security ceiling does not limit this Medicare component. |
| FUTA rate, section 3301 | 6.2 percent | The earlier 6.0 percent rate does not govern. |
| FUTA annual base, section 3306(b)(1) | $7,000 per recipient in this employer's annual account | Its unchanged amount does not merge different years; the obsolete $3,000 printed regulatory figure cannot supersede the statute. |

**Controlling explanation:** The Court applies the rate and base rules independently. Sections 31.3111-3 and 31.3111-2(c) address FICA attachment and rate, while section 31.3121(a)(1)-1(a)(2) supplies the separate annual-base framework. Sections 31.3301-2 and 31.3301-3(b), together with section 31.3306(b)(1)-1(a)(2), perform the corresponding FUTA work. Their established backpay interpretation places the payments in 1994 for each inquiry. The governing statute controls the current dollar ceiling when an older regulatory print states an obsolete amount.

The earlier wage maxima explain why the allocation matters for all but one recipient. They do not establish the exceptional recipient's final liability, convert Medicare's unchanged percentage into an unchanged tax, or fix a net tax from the displayed statutory percentages alone. The lower court must implement these legal rules on the settled payment record, rather than award a refund on the rejected earlier-year theory.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The judgment fixes the applicable allocation, rates and bases, not an unsupported final dollar computation. Employee credits, benefits classification, any employee election and unpresented interest payments are outside this employer refund action. No speculative factual hearing is required merely to repeat the agreed payment dates.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Social Security Board v. Nierotko:** Preserved for wage classification and allocation to earlier benefit periods, including its consideration of paid-quarter language. Its benefit judgment does not compel the same allocation under the later employer-tax provisions, and its restriction of agencies to lawful statutory authority remains effective.
- **Auer v. Robbins:** Applied to the responsible agency's fair, considered and textually permissible construction of its own regulations; no new general deference rule is adopted.
- **Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala:** Applied to the agency's actual established ground, without supplying a fictional interpretation or overlooking the statutory boundary.
- **Christensen v. Harris County:** Preserved for separate treatment of advisory statutory views and regulatory interpretation, and for the prohibition on altering clear regulatory text by interpretation. A revenue ruling's publication alone does not settle its independent force.
- **Gitlitz v. Commissioner:** Its express classification and sequencing commands remain effective. A stipulation of wage status does not resolve this distinct temporal-attribution question or authorize a policy override of clear tax law.

**Resulting law:** The existing lawful Treasury construction governs payment-year employer taxation of these back wages under the applicable separate rate and annual-base provisions. The controlling rationale depends on a fair, established regulatory interpretation within statutory bounds, and does not adopt independent statutory compulsion as its sufficient ground. Benefit attribution remains governed by its own law. The judgment preserves actual-or-constructive-payment qualifications and does not establish an earlier payment, deferred-compensation plan or employee election.

### United States v. Weston — 2001-04-17

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/United_States_v_Weston_merits_2001-04-17.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/United_States_v_Weston_merits_2001-04-17.md#precedent-treatment).

#### I. The present safety justification does not support unwanted medication

**Holding and operative rule:** When a pretrial defendant objects to antipsychotic medication, the Government must establish medical appropriateness and an overriding need after considering reasonable less intrusive means. A safety label does not satisfy that duty where the evidence establishes present containment of immediate danger and the order does not adequately address that alternative; the present authorization therefore cannot stand.

**Authority:** O'Connor's Part I is directly joined by all nine Justices. It independently supports affirmance.

**Controlling explanation:** Riggins v. Nevada requires a supported justification for overriding bodily refusal, rather than medical benefit alone. Washington v. Harper recognizes a substantial refusal interest while permitting justified safety treatment; it does not make diagnosis or potential dangerousness conclusive. Here the treating psychiatrist acknowledged that isolation and continuous observation controlled immediate danger. The risk during staff contact and the medical and practical costs of prolonged isolation are substantial competing considerations, but the order must assess them against the actual alternatives. The Court does not find indefinite isolation harmless, permanently preferable or constitutionally required. Nor does prosecutorial good faith supply the missing safety justification when restoration is an acknowledged purpose. The second administrative hearing received defense material; the defect of the first hearing does not establish that the second lacked every opportunity for input. A future order supported by materially changed circumstances or an adequate independent safety showing remains possible. This order lacks the required justification.

#### II. The restoration purpose requires a preliminary assessment of likely defense effects on this record

**Holding and operative rule:** When competency restoration is an actual purpose of proposed unwanted medication and the record supplies no independently sufficient safety justification, the judge must assess the proposed regimen's reasonably foreseeable effects on comprehension, consultation, testimony, demeanor and presentation of the defense before authorizing it. The Government must support that preliminary assessment through available clinical evidence, reasonable alternatives and workable safeguards; perfect prediction is unnecessary, but a promise to examine effects after treatment does not suffice, and actual effects require renewed review before trial.

**Authority:** O'Connor's Part II is directly joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer. Scalia and Thomas do not join it. Seven direct joins establish the stated rule without a Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** Riggins recognizes that medication can impair the substance of consultation and testimony in ways a completed trial record cannot reconstruct. That protection would be ineffective if all defense effects could be postponed until after the intervention intended to permit prosecution. The Government correctly observes that exact effects cannot always be known beforehand. The required inquiry therefore considers the proposed treatment, clinical observations and supported inferences, not a guarantee against every side effect. An insanity defense does not invariably entitle a defendant to display untreated symptoms. Recordings and psychiatric explanation may preserve evidence of earlier symptoms, and dose changes may help; their adequacy must be assessed, not presumed. They cannot necessarily restore impaired communication or preparation. Harper's safety setting does not answer the deficient mixed-purpose order before the Court. The judge remains responsible for the legal trial-rights determination. This necessary preliminary inquiry is not a comprehensive sufficient standard for ultimate restoration authorization.

**Limits and work on remand:** The clinical findings for any renewed regimen remain factual work under these rules. Whether prosecution alone can ultimately justify involuntary treatment is outside the granted timing question. Immediate emergency treatment, treatment solely for execution, capital medical ethics, guilt, discharge and dismissal are not decided. No majority rule is adopted here for a materially different order resting on an independently sufficient safety basis; Stone-Zsela's broader position and Kennedy's stronger ultimate standard remain separate.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Riggins v. Nevada is applied to the present refusal-interest defect and extended, at the seven-Justice scope, to the timing of a preliminary defense-effects inquiry before restoration treatment. Its complete ultimate-restoration question remains open.
- Washington v. Harper retains its medically justified confinement-safety rule; its setting and procedure do not automatically answer pretrial restoration and defense protection. No categorical safety-treatment prohibition is adopted.

**Resulting law:** An objecting pretrial defendant retains the Riggins liberty protection. For a restoration-purpose order without an independently sufficient safety justification, the judge must also assess likely defense effects before treatment, using supported clinical prediction and safeguards and preserving later reassessment. The two inquiries are distinct. These requirements govern renewed authorization, not merely review after a conviction. They neither establish a right to remain incompetent nor decide a complete substantive restoration standard.

### Easley v. Cromartie / Smallwood v. Cromartie — 2001-04-18

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Easley_v_Cromartie_Smallwood_v_Cromartie_merits_2001-04-18.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Easley_v_Cromartie_Smallwood_v_Cromartie_merits_2001-04-18.md#precedent-treatment).

#### Racial predominance cannot cure the absence of a cognizable districting injury

**Holding and operative rule:** A common territorial district cannot be invalidated solely because racial considerations predominated, even when ordinary districting criteria were subordinated. The court must identify an actually presented and preserved exclusion, intentional dilution, materially unequal participation, inferior civic membership or other independently cognizable deprivation, apply that claim's own elements, burdens and justification rules, and otherwise enter judgment for defendants on the insufficient design-only theory.

**Authority:** Ginsburg's opinion is joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, with the author counted once within those five. The four other Justices support only immediate vacatur, not this rule or remand. Five direct joins control; no Marks synthesis is needed.

**Controlling explanation:** Shaw v. Reno distinguishes the racial composition of a common electorate from independently unequal treatment. Johnson v. Miller applies that distinction even when predominance and subordination of ordinary criteria are established. Hunt v. Cromartie made the same distinction the mandate in this case. A fuller finding about a legally insufficient premise cannot supply the missing constitutional wrong. Rule 52's deference to factual findings does not determine their legal consequence.

District shape, race-politics correlation, drafting communications, testimony, maps and electoral experience may be relevant to a cognizable claim. They are neither an automatic injury nor evidence that must be disregarded. No statistical model or particular alternative map is a universal entry requirement; any evidence required by the actual claim remains necessary. A partisan explanation does not defeat a separately proved wrong. Likewise, unequal treatment itself can complete an injury without a second governmental act, electoral defeat or financial loss.

The record establishes that the district court relied on predominance, but it does not establish whether a separate claim was actually preserved and proved under Hunt's mandate. The proper consequence is legal vacatur with confined review of any such claim, using existing findings to the extent material and ordinary procedural rules. It is neither a new claim nor a source-based finding of waiver.

**Limits and remand work:** Existence, preservation, proof and appropriate relief for an independent claim require the actual record. No final plan-validity ruling, replacement-plan statutory condition, new map or election remedy is decided. An unpleaded theory is outside the preserved case; no presumption establishes that all independent claims were omitted. Ordinary application of the controlling rule remains distinct from a legal question left unanswered.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Shaw v. Reno is reaffirmed: territorial racial composition alone is insufficient, while independently unequal treatment remains actionable under its own rules.
- Johnson v. Miller is reaffirmed and applied to the legal consequence of a completed predominance finding; its ordinary evidentiary rules and no-second-loss qualification remain.
- Hunt v. Cromartie is enforced: its remand did not direct a predominance-only trial and cannot be displaced by completing one.
- Bush v. Vera retains the same territorial-design boundary and protection of independently cognizable claims.
- Adarand Constructors v. Peña remains the justification framework for an actual covered racial allocation; it does not supply the missing cause of action from territorial design.

**Resulting law:** Predominance alone remains insufficient in territorial districting challenges after trial as well as at pleading or summary judgment. Courts must connect purpose findings to a cognizable claim and provide relief only for a proved wrong under that claim's elements. Ordinary factual deference cannot convert an insufficient theory into a constitutional deprivation. The decision preserves actual exclusion, dilution, unequal participation and civic-membership claims without requiring a later additional loss.

### Shaw v. Murphy — 2001-04-18

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Shaw_v_Murphy_merits_2001-04-18.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Shaw_v_Murphy_merits_2001-04-18.md#precedent-treatment).

#### Legal advice supplies no categorical enhancement of inmate speech protection

**Holding and operative rule:** An inmate-to-inmate communication receives no heightened constitutional standard merely because it offers legal advice, including when advice and criticism are mixed. A restriction remains subject to Turner's reasonable relationship to legitimate penological interests, while actual obstruction of meaningful access and independently proved retaliation are assessed under their own rules; rejecting enhancement does not itself validate the discipline.

**Authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court is joined in full by all nine Justices. The same direct coalition adopts the remand and its limits.

**Controlling explanation:** Turner v. Safley examines a valid rational connection to a legitimate and neutral prison objective, alternatives for exercising the right, accommodation's effects on others and prison resources, and ready alternatives to the restriction. Its deference is contextual; an unsupported administrative assertion is not conclusive. The legal-advice label does not determine a communication's actual function or security effect and cannot create a separate constitutional tier.

Johnson v. Avery protects necessary assistance, and Lewis v. Casey requires effective access arrangements under its actual-obstruction rule. Neither guarantees a preferred adviser or a content-based privilege for every communication. Necessary help remains relevant to a proper access claim and to the practical operation of the restriction. Murphy's independent speech claim does not require proof that a recipient lost a lawsuit. Pearson v. Hines separately protects against proved retaliatory fabrication; no such finding is made here. Denius v. Dunlap's public-employment consultation rule does not displace prison-specific Turner analysis.

Another clerk was offered and security classifications barred Murphy's assignment to Tracy. Those facts matter but do not conclusively establish the validity of this discipline. The insolence provision concerns intended harassment or alarm and examples directed to employees. There is no finding that this letter was directed to Galle or intended to harass him; Murphy's statement that he did not anticipate Galle reading it was uncontradicted. These circumstances are not affirmative findings that prison administration could suffer no legitimate harm. Conversely, officials' ability to read correspondence does not establish the missing intent. The actual letter, rule construction, supported interests and available alternatives require ordinary adjudication.

**Limits and remand work:** The grant addresses the asserted special right, not conclusive adjudication of Turner's application or the preserved as-applied vagueness and overbreadth claims. Those remain for decision on the record. No lost qualifying claim, fabricated retaliation, new access claimant or actual entitlement to relief is presumed. Any prospective remedy must satisfy the applicable statutory requirements.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Turner v. Safley is applied without a special legal-advice enhancement; its contextual deference and four considerations remain operative.
- Johnson v. Avery retains protection for genuinely necessary assistance, not an automatic adviser privilege.
- Lewis v. Casey preserves effective access, the actual-obstruction rule and independent communication claims; it does not supply a lost-lawsuit prerequisite for Murphy's speech claim.
- Pearson v. Hines remains the rule for proved retaliatory fabrication under its own protected-conduct, causation and same-decision requirements; no violation is found here.
- Denius v. Dunlap is distinguished at its public-employment scope and creates no categorical prison exception.

**Resulting law:** Legal-advice content does not increase the constitutional tier for inmate correspondence. Courts still examine the actual regulation and application under Turner and preserve distinct access, grievance-retaliation and due-process claims under their own elements. The absence of an enhanced tier gives officials no immunity for unsupported restrictions or proven fabrication and ends no duty to supply assistance necessary for effective access.

### Children's Healthcare Is a Legal Duty, Inc. v. Min De Parle — 2001-04-23

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Childrens_Healthcare_Is_a_Legal_Duty_v_Min_De_Parle_merits_2001-04-23.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Childrens_Healthcare_Is_a_Legal_Duty_v_Min_De_Parle_merits_2001-04-23.md#precedent-treatment).

#### I. This limited secular-care accommodation is permissible in its terms

**Holding and operative rule:** Congress may accommodate religious refusal of medical treatment by reimbursing the independently identifiable nonmedical component of an existing health benefit for patients whose physical condition otherwise qualifies, subject to the enacted coverage limits and effective public control. The Constitution does not require this limited accommodation to include every comparable secular objection; the permission does not authorize devotional services, a subsidy entitlement or every current application.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part I has five direct joins: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer, including the author. The four other Justices dissent from this component.

**Controlling explanation:** Corporation of Presiding Bishop v. Amos permits accommodation beyond constitutional compulsion. Kiryas Joel preserves that permission while prohibiting denominational allocation of governmental authority and an anomalous denominational franchise. This program removes a religious obstacle to receiving only the secular component of otherwise qualifying care; it does not confer the governmental franchise condemned there. The religious condition is a serious distinction, and removing a denomination's name cannot alone justify it. Its support here is the limited relationship between the physical need, the existing benefit and the refusal of the medical component. Agostini requires examination of the governmental function, not a presumption that religious motivation makes physical care devotional. Mitchell likewise distinguishes actual financed use from institutional identity or possible diversion. These principles permit the stated accommodation while requiring separate review of the care actually purchased and the control actually exercised. The holding does not make all religion-specific benefits valid or establish that the present program operates lawfully in every application.

#### II. Initial institutional review does not necessarily transfer final public power

**Holding and operative rule:** An institution's initial coverage recommendation does not unconstitutionally delegate final governmental authority when public officials retain independent responsibility to determine statutory eligibility, may require sufficient objective information and may deny payment on an inadequate showing. Exemption from compelled religiously objectionable medical procedures does not eliminate the condition standard, all usable nonmedical evidence or review of billed services; formal retention of authority does not establish its meaningful operation in every application.

**Authority:** All nine directly join Breyer's Part II. This statutory-facial conclusion is independent of the eligibility and operational disagreements.

**Controlling explanation:** Larkin v. Grendel's Den prohibits the transfer of a final public licensing veto to religious bodies. Kiryas Joel prohibits denominational allocation of civil governmental authority. Neither equates an initial provider recommendation with final public power. Here utilization review, a requirement to supply sufficient condition information, review of covered services and final governmental responsibility remain distinct. Nonmedical observations, records and other reliable evidence may establish the physical condition without a compelled medical examination. A claim lacking sufficient information may be denied; the exemption does not make institutional religious judgment conclusive. The separate protection against religiously contrary medical supervision also preserves review for noncovered, excessive or fraudulent billing. The objection that public review could become nominal concerns operation and remains legally material. It does not establish inevitable delegation from the enacted terms alone.

#### III. The operational judgment requires assessment under the actual-function rules

**Holding and operative rule:** Summary judgment on a supported challenge to actual public financing or religious control must evaluate the evidence and reasonable supported inferences under the financed-function and public-control rules, rather than infer compliance from a secular description or formally retained authority. The operational judgment is vacated for that bounded assessment; religious affiliation, private prayer, possible diversion and a source gap alone establish neither a violation nor a duty to reopen evidence.

**Authority:** Breyer's Part III is directly joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, including the author. Stone-Zsela supplies an additional judgment vote only. The five direct joins, not an aggregation of separate grounds, control.

**Controlling explanation:** Agostini requires identification of what government purchases and who controls it. Mitchell distinguishes enforceable safeguards from their observance, demands an actual financed-use connection and preserves independently lawful applications. Marshfield confirms that a genuine formal change does not end the inquiry into continuing public conduct, although its particular monument facts supply no benefits rule. The Cook affidavit and earlier manual supply concrete evidence to assess for linkage and materiality; the lower court's independent Lemon-prong analysis and generalized characterizations do not complete that inquiry. The contrary objection is substantial: prayer in a religious institution does not itself show that public money purchased it, and the manual retains final public responsibility. The remand respects those limits. It finds neither financed devotion nor a surrendered public decision, shifts no ordinary burden and requires no new evidence where the existing materials suffice. The courts must decide the supported operational claims under the correct rule and fit any eventual relief to a proved function while preserving lawful applications.

#### Statutory conditions preserved by these holdings

Medicare payment requires an effective election and a condition that would qualify for the corresponding inpatient-hospital or extended-care benefit at an ordinary hospital or skilled nursing facility. The signed election states conscientious opposition to nonexcepted medical treatment and inconsistency with sincere religious belief. Excepted treatment means treatment received involuntarily or required by federal, state or local law. An election ordinarily takes effect upon execution and continues until revoked. Written voluntary notice revokes it; nonexcepted treatment reimbursed under Medicare produces statutory deemed revocation. The form separately warns that receipt of nonexcepted medical services revokes the election and may restrict future services. After two elections and revocations, the next election waits one year from the most recent revocation and later elections wait five years. No individual compliance or revocation fact is found.

Qualifying institutions must be tax exempt, lawfully operated, supply the defined physical care exclusively through experienced nonmedical personnel on a 24-hour inpatient basis, and satisfy the religious-service and medical-provider ownership and affiliation restrictions. Ownership interests below five percent are disregarded. The separate affiliation exceptions cover uncompensated governing-body service, a director, trustee, officer, employee or staff member of the nonmedical institution having a family relationship with an individual affiliated with, or holding an ownership interest in, a medical-treatment provider, and vendors serving both types of providers. Accreditation may substitute only for conditions for which the Secretary finds reasonable assurance of compliance. Utilization committees review admissions, duration, extended stays and services, keep records and satisfy additional necessary review requirements. Institutions must supply required coverage and quality information and meet necessary health-and-safety requirements. The patient examination exemption preserves the sufficient-condition-information requirement; the distinct institutional supervision exemption preserves billing review for noncoverage, excess and fraud.

The Secretary must estimate expenditures before each fiscal year beginning with fiscal 2000 and monitor them beginning with fiscal 1999. If the estimate exceeds the statutory trigger, proportional payment reduction is required, subject to the Secretary's option to impose alternative coverage conditions or limitations sufficient to meet the trigger. The unadjusted trigger starts at $20 million for fiscal 1998 and increases by the prescribed annual consumer-price index; the next year's trigger is reduced or increased by the previous excess or deficit, with a $50 million ceiling on an increase. Permitted patient charges increase by the reduction amount. The review restriction concerns the identified expenditure estimates and reductions, not every coverage determination. After three consecutive excessive-expenditure years beginning no earlier than fiscal 2002, payment from the next January 1 is limited to patients with elections then effective and only for those elections' duration. No expenditure or sunset trigger is established here. Medicaid's statutory substitutions and supervision exemptions require their own application; Medicare's election mechanism is not silently made the entire Medicaid regime.

**Limits and remaining work:** The enactment applies to services furnished from August 5, 1997; its July 1, 1998 deadline for initial regulations and recognition of good-faith elections before those regulations took effect do not establish actual compliance. The actual financed-service and review connection remains for adjudication, not an unresolved constitutional standard. The Court neither decides individual sincerity or coverage claims nor orders severability relief against the upheld facial criterion.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Corporation of Presiding Bishop v. Amos supplies permission for noncompulsory accommodation at the limited secular-benefit scope; it is not a universal subsidy rule.
- Kiryas Joel retains its civil-authority, denominational-neutrality and accommodation boundaries; the program's statutory recommendation is distinguished from its general governmental franchise.
- Larkin v. Grendel's Den retains its prohibition on a religious final civil veto and is distinguished from initial recommendations subject to public decisions.
- Agostini v. Felton governs through the modified unified Lemon inquiry, preserving distinct doctrines and operational secular-service controls. Former independent Lemon prongs are not revived.
- Mitchell v. Helms supplies actual financed-use proof, the difference between statutory controls and compliance, and fitted relief preserving lawful applications.
- Freedom From Religion Foundation v. City of Marshfield supplies examination of actual public conduct despite a genuine formal change; its monument-specific outcome is not transferred to benefits.

**Resulting law:** The enacted religious nonmedical-care accommodation and initial recommendation structure survive the facial challenges at the stated conditions. Public payment must still purchase independently identifiable secular work and remain under meaningful public authority. Statutory qualification, beneficiary eligibility, a covered service and an actual payment are distinct. The courts must reassess the supported operational claims under those rules without presumed diversion or presumed compliance. Stone-Zsela's equal-consideration extension and the three Associates' financial-preference theory do not become governing law.

### Clark County School District v. Breeden — 2001-04-23

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Clark_County_School_District_v_Breeden_summary-disposition_2001-04-23.md#holdings); [precedent treatment](../records/Clark_County_School_District_v_Breeden_summary-disposition_2001-04-23.md#precedent-treatment).

**Common authority:** The Court's per curiam opinion states both controlling grounds below. No individual lineup is published.

#### 1. This report-review exchange does not support the asserted reasonable belief

**Holding and operative rule:** Even assuming that objectively reasonable good-faith opposition to a perceived Title VII violation is protected before a violation is proved, this particular isolated exchange during assigned review of an applicant's psychological report cannot support the asserted reasonable belief that an unlawful hostile environment existed. This is a contextual application, not a rule excluding every single incident or requiring employees to wait for completed harassment before raising a reasonable concern.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the exchange must be evaluated in its actual work setting. Harris asks whether the conditions are objectively and subjectively hostile, considering their severity, frequency, threatening or humiliating character and interference with work. Psychological injury and lost productivity are not prerequisites. An extremely serious isolated act can qualify; the ordinary rule is severity or pervasiveness, not a mandatory number of incidents.

The employees were performing assigned review of an applicant's report. Breeden did not object to reading the reported remark itself. Her objection concerns the supervisor's response, a coworker's offer to explain it and their laughter. In that context the incident was neither sufficiently serious nor part of an established continuing abusive condition to support the asserted objectively reasonable belief. Subjective offense alone does not supply the objective threshold.

An employee may reasonably complain about a developing unlawful practice without waiting for repeated mistreatment. That principle does not supply a supported pattern, threatened continuation or extreme act absent from this record. Nor does a supervisor's participation answer the antecedent environment question. Faragher's actual-authority rule concerns attribution of an established wrong; it does not turn every supervisory remark into one. The Court assumes the favorable reasonable-belief protection and does not adopt a broader limitation on opposition or participation whenever the underlying claim fails.

**Limits and matters not reached:** The general outer limits of reasonable-belief opposition are unnecessary to this judgment and remain undecided. No separate hostile-environment liability claim or new exhaustion requirement is adjudicated.

#### 2. The supplied chronology does not establish retaliatory transfer

**Holding and operative rule:** Implementation of a transfer already contemplated before the relevant decisionmaker learned of suit does not, by timing alone, support retaliation where no evidence shows a retaliatory change or acceleration; the twenty-month interval from the earlier administrative charge likewise supplies no sufficient causal inference by itself here. A preexisting plan is not conclusive immunity, and a long interval is not an absolute bar when other evidence supports causation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the timing must be measured against actual knowledge and the prior plan. Rice contemplated transfer on April 10, before service on April 11, and her unchallenged affidavit states that she learned of the action only after April 11. Completing the transfer in May does not by itself show that the suit caused the already contemplated step. The district-court presentation relied entirely on chronology; it supplied no supported retaliatory alteration, admission or other causal evidence. The August 1995 charge, approximately twenty months earlier, does not fill that gap.

The right-to-sue letter theory appeared only in the appellate reply, not below or in the opening brief. It does not establish Rice's knowledge of the letter or a new protected employee act. The requirement that the employer receive notice of a charge within ten days is a legal command, not proof that Rice or her predecessor actually received it. The Court finds neither receipt nor nonreceipt from that command. Speculation about the letter therefore cannot sustain reversal of summary judgment.

Testimony, explanations, admissions and circumstantial evidence can support a retaliation claim; statistical proof is unnecessary. An initially legitimate action can later be implemented for retaliatory reasons. Those possibilities lack evidentiary support here, and the mere sequence of protected proceedings and completed transfer cannot replace the missing causal link. The Court assumes the formal administrative and litigation activity is protected and adopts no new comprehensive causation formula.

**Limits and matters not reached:** No universal temporal cutoff, automatic notice presumption, categorical prior-plan defense or later retaliation standard is adopted. A materially different supported implementation theory would require facts absent here; none is invented for remand.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Harris v. Forklift Systems, Inc.:** Applied contextually to objective and subjective hostility, severity or pervasiveness and the rejection of mandatory psychological injury. The decision does not require repeated incidents regardless of seriousness or completed liability before every reasonable complaint.
- **Oncale v. Sundowner Offshore Services, Inc.:** Preserved for attention to social context and the distinction between actionable discrimination and ordinary offensive behavior; no new restriction on protected complaints follows.
- **Faragher v. City of Boca Raton:** Its actual-authority/material-aid attribution rule remains effective for an established wrong. Supervisory status does not supply a missing hostile environment or retaliation link, and no different affirmative defense is imported.

**Resulting law:** Both presented retaliation theories fail on their actual records. Reasonable opposition is assumed protected without requiring ultimate success on the underlying claim; this isolated exchange supplies no objectively reasonable basis of the asserted kind. Transfer timing alone supplies no retaliation inference in light of the prior contemplation and knowledge evidence. Contextual hostility, actual attribution and causal proof remain separate; no complaint-count formula, universal temporal cutoff or conclusive prior-plan defense replaces them.

### Alexander v. Sandoval — 2001-04-24

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Alexander_v_Sandoval_merits_2001-04-24.md#holdings).

#### Prospective enforcement of Title VI's valid implementing requirements

**Holding and operative rule:** Title VI's established private action permits statutory beneficiaries to obtain prospective relief requiring a covered recipient to comply with valid, sufficiently definite section 602 regulations implementing the statutory protection against discrimination, including the effects regulation assumed valid here. Congress supplies the action through Title VI read as a whole; a regulation cannot independently create an action under an otherwise remediless statute, and plaintiffs must prove the regulation's actual elements and obtain relief fitted to the violation rather than rely on statistical imbalance alone.

**Authority:** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls this rule, its explanation, the application and remedy, and the treatment of Powell below. These are five direct joins at the stated level of generality.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress's protection and its authorized implementation operate together. Section 601 identifies the persons protected from discrimination in federally assisted programs; section 602 expressly directs implementation of that protection. Cannon supplies the established private-action setting and a contextual inquiry into congressional authorization. Congress's 1986 recognition of legal and equitable remedies in Title VI suits and the Civil Rights Restoration Act's preservation of the enforcement structure provide affirmative statutory context, not an inference from silence alone. Read together, these provisions support prospective enforcement of valid, definite administration requirements protecting those beneficiaries.

Petitioners correctly distinguish an agency's authority to impose a duty from Congress's authorization of a judicial action. But section 602's agency-facing language does not sever its implementing standards from the private action accepted within this particular statutory scheme. Administrative funding enforcement is not made the exclusive means of securing compliance. The additional effects obligation does not become an intentional-discrimination claim under section 601; prospective enforcement rests on the integrated statute. Guardians and Choate explain that substantive distinction without themselves conclusively deciding this action.

McClellan requires contextual authorization and rejects an unsupported Cable Act damages action; it does not impose an express-action-clause prerequisite or erase Title VI's established enforcement background. Central Bank preserves a confined received securities action and confirms the need to examine the particular scheme, without deciding this Title VI question. Franklin separates an action's existence from available relief; an injunction request does not supply its own authority. The practical loss of an effective individual remedy confirms the consequences of the competing readings, but does not create the action.

The Department receives federal assistance. The regulations' validity and the finding of unjustified discriminatory effects are accepted premises of this review. Sandoval may maintain this prospective action, and the reviewed injunction remains supported by that route.

#### Powell's direct-action restriction is overruled to the stated extent

**Holding and operative rule:** Powell v. Ridge is overruled insofar as its direct-action holding forecloses the prospective Title VI enforcement just described. Its distinct section 1983 holding is not decided or abrogated, and today's judgment neither authorizes retrospective damages for unintentional discrimination nor disturbs a completed final judgment.

**Authority:** The same five Justices join this express partial overruling and its complete statutory stare-decisis explanation in the Opinion of the Court.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court does not distinguish Powell on the ground that Sandoval seeks an injunction. Powell itself rejected prospective enforcement of the additional effects obligation. Nor do a different state policy or a finding instead of an allegation of effects change its action-creation rule. Reconsideration is necessary.

Statutory stare decisis deserves substantial weight because Congress can correct an interpretation and courts ordinarily preserve settled constructions. Powell nevertheless gave insufficient weight to the integrated source of Title VI's accepted beneficiary action. It treated the difference between statutory intentional-discrimination liability and valid preventive implementation as dispositive of prospective enforcement, although that difference does not answer the statute-specific authorization question. The Court corrects that identified error in the enforcement arrangement, rather than making disagreement alone a reason to overrule precedent.

Powell is recent, and no concrete reliance arrangement has been identified here. Neither consideration independently permits overruling a statutory decision. Their significance is that covered recipients already owed the regulatory duties whose prospective enforcement was barred. Restoring that enforcement channel does not prescribe a new substantive language policy, impose retrospective damages, or reopen a final judgment. Reliance on the absence of a beneficiary's action does not outweigh the better-supported statutory arrangement in these circumstances. The Chief Justice acknowledges that he joined Powell's contrary holding.

The Court preserves Powell's distinction between regulatory validity and the source of a private remedy, while rejecting its conclusion about this prospective Title VI action. Powell's separate section 1983 analysis concerns a distinct enforcement vehicle and individual statutory-right inquiry. That question is outside this grant and remains untouched.

#### Application, remedy and limits

The Eleventh Circuit's judgment is affirmed insofar as it recognizes this private prospective action and sustains the injunction requiring accommodation of non-English speakers in driver's-license examinations. The injunction remains operative on the accepted findings and within the scope reviewed. No damages are awarded and no regulation is invalidated.

Regulatory validity, the correctness of the disparate-impact merits determination, damages availability for unintentional discrimination, and a separate section 1983 action are outside the granted question. The accepted section 601 action for intentional discrimination remains, with its own intent requirement; this judgment finds no intentional discrimination. Ordinary application of the announced rule requires proof of the actual regulatory elements, coverage and fitted relief. Those applications are work under the governing rule, not a reservation about whether this category of prospective enforcement exists. The Court creates no new claim, funding cutoff, private administrative-exhaustion requirement or statistical presumption.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Powell v. Ridge, April 3, 2000:** Its direct-action prohibition is overruled only to the extent it bars prospective enforcement of valid, sufficiently definite Title VI implementing requirements protecting statutory beneficiaries. Its separate section 1983 holding, the distinction between regulatory validity and remedy authorization, and its completed judgment remain undisturbed.
- **Cannon v. University of Chicago:** Its contextual inquiry and established Title VI/Title IX private-action setting supply the foundation for the integrated statutory interpretation; Cannon is not treated as having already decided this regulatory action.
- **Guardians Association v. Civil Service Commission:** Its distinction between intentional-discrimination liability and regulatory effects protection is preserved. Its separate positions and remedial discussion are not converted into an earlier controlling resolution of the present action.
- **Alexander v. Choate:** Its account of the statutory/regulatory distinction remains; that distinction does not by itself answer prospective enforcement under Title VI as a whole.
- **McClellan v. Cablevision of Connecticut, Inc., January 7, 1999:** Its requirement of contextual congressional authorization is applied. Its rejection of the particular Cable Act damages action remains, distinguished by that statute's different remedial background rather than by a rule that requests for injunctions create actions.
- **Central Bank of Denver, N.A. v. First Interstate Bank of Denver, N.A., April 19, 1994:** Its confined received securities action remains intact; its contextual received-action rationale is consistent with, but does not itself establish, the Title VI rule adopted here.
- **Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, February 26, 1992:** Its separation of an existing action from appropriate relief is applied. Its intentional-discrimination damages holding does not decide damages for unintentional effects here.
- **Blessing v. Freestone, April 21, 1997:** Its separate individual-right and section 1983 enforcement inquiries remain. The present Title VI direct-action holding supplies no new section 1983 ruling.

**Resulting law:** Effective April 24, 2001, Title VI's established private action permits statutory beneficiaries to enforce prospectively valid, sufficiently definite section 602 requirements implementing their protection against discrimination. This includes valid effects regulations of the kind presented here. Plaintiffs must establish the regulation's actual elements and obtain relief fitted to the violation; statistical imbalance alone is not automatically enough.

Powell's contrary direct-action restriction no longer governs that category of prospective claims. Its separate section 1983 holding remains, as do section 601's intentional-discrimination requirement, valid administrative enforcement and the distinction between a regulatory duty and the congressional source of a private action. This decision neither creates private actions under otherwise remediless statutes nor resolves damages for unintentional effects. Its rule governs covered claims; it does not reopen completed final judgments.

### Atwater v. City of Lago Vista — 2001-04-24

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Atwater_v_City_of_Lago_Vista_merits_2001-04-24.md#holdings).

#### Additional custody for a known fine-only criminal offense

**Holding and operative rule:** Probable cause for an observed fine-only criminal misdemeanor does not, by itself, justify additional station-house transport and confinement. That added custody must reasonably serve an actual enforcement function, considering supported identification, appearance, safety, continuing-violation, evidence-protection or necessary-processing needs; the examples are nonexclusive, are not presumed, and require neither the least intrusive imaginable alternative nor citation whenever conceivable.

**Authority:** Part I of Ginsburg's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor and Breyer, supplies five direct joins to this rule, the rejection of categorical summary judgment and the common mandate. The rule extends Ricci's civil-licensing holding to the fine-only criminal enforcement presented here.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that authority to stop a person and the reasonable extent of custody are different questions. Ricci requires an actual enforcement connection for added station custody after a fine-only civil violation. The criminal label does not, by itself, justify the same added restraint where imprisonment is unavailable as punishment. The State's penalty choice reinforces the need to assess the scale of the seizure, without granting immunity from arrest.

Whren preserves an ordinary initial stop on objective probable cause while retaining independent limits on greater restraint. Knowles keeps search consequences separate from the antecedent lawful-arrest question. Robinson and Gustafson presuppose a qualifying lawful arrest; their incident-search rules cannot supply the missing justification for disputed custody. Wilson v. Arkansas treats relevant history as part of reasonableness without constitutionalizing every state authorization. A categorical historical breach-of-peace rule is unnecessary to decide this actual-function question.

Clear administration is a legitimate interest. But optional booking paperwork cannot conclusively establish the need for the custody that creates it. The inquiry permits practical judgments without hindsight perfection or proof that every alternative would fail. The lower court's probable-cause rule omitted that inquiry.

The record must include both the missing physical documents and the supplied license number, address and known local residence. The earlier warning followed recognition that the child was belted; it does not establish another seatbelt offense. The friend took the children. These facts cannot be replaced by assumptions of failed verification, flight risk, continuing danger or compulsory station processing. Neither berating nor a routine booking preference alone supplies an enforcement need. The correct rule defeats defendants' categorical entitlement to judgment, while the Justices differ on the ultimate application and the remaining record determinations.

#### The common judgment does not establish a controlling application conclusion

**Judgment and operative rule:** The Fifth Circuit judgment sustaining the categorical custody summary judgment is vacated, and the custody claim returns for proceedings under the actual-enforcement rule. The return permits genuinely supported disputes and preserved defenses, requires no new evidentiary hearing, invents no enforcement justification and reopens no established fact by speculation; it does not invalidate the uncontested seatbelt convictions or award damages.

**Authority and application limits:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer expressly support this vacatur and remand. Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer also conclude that the supported plaintiff-favorable account does not establish a sufficient custody function and would reverse on that ground. Stone-Zsela does not join that application: he requires actual verification and custody-need determinations under the rule. Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas find the arrest reasonable on their stated grounds and would affirm. There is no majority holding that this custody was constitutional or unconstitutional; the four-Justice application and the distinct concurrence cannot be combined through Marks into one.

Personal qualified immunity remains a separate conduct-date inquiry using March 1997 law; Ricci, Knowles, McArthur and this decision supply no retrospective notice. Municipal liability separately requires an attributable policy or custom, applicable fault, causation and injury. No final finding resolves those predicates. Other punishment classes are outside the presented question. Ordinary application of the rule and liability work under it are distinct from the Court's lack of a controlling answer on this custody's ultimate reasonableness.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Ricci v. Village of Arlington Heights, May 4, 1998:** Its actual-enforcement requirement is extended from fine-only civil licensing custody to the added custody for a known fine-only criminal offense. Its rejection of categorical arrest immunity, least restrictive means and a universal citation duty remains. Its record-specific application fracture supplies no automatic application answer here.
- **Whren v. United States, June 10, 1996:** Its objective initial-stop rule is preserved and its independent limits on greater restraint applied. Hostility does not replace the objective enforcement inquiry.
- **Knowles v. Iowa, December 8, 1998:** Its distinction between actual lawful custody and search consequences is applied; it supplies no authority to justify an arrest by its ensuing search or booking effects.
- **United States v. Robinson; Gustafson v. Florida:** Their incident-search rules remain conditioned on a qualifying lawful arrest. Neither decides the contested antecedent custody authority here.
- **Wilson v. Arkansas, May 22, 1995:** Relevant history remains part of constitutional reasonableness, without turning state arrest authorization into a complete federal justification.
- **Illinois v. McArthur, February 20, 2001:** Its particularized residential-preservation holding remains at that scope. Concrete need and fitted execution inform the comparison; its home setting and approximate duration create no traffic-booking permission or safe harbor.
- **Wilson v. Layne; Wallis v. Spencer:** Conduct-date immunity and independent municipal responsibility remain distinct from constitutional merits. Neither supplies a present finding of immunity, City policy, fault or causation here.

**Resulting law:** Ricci's actual-enforcement rule now governs added station transport and confinement for known fine-only criminal offenses as well as its prior civil setting. Probable cause and local arrest permission alone do not establish reasonableness; genuinely supported enforcement needs and the scale of restraint must be assessed. Neither a universal citation requirement nor categorical arrest immunity follows.

The Court supplies no controlling application conclusion about this custody, immunity or municipal liability. The four-Justice conclusion that the accepted account is constitutionally insufficient remains noncontrolling; the actual-function rule and bounded corrective mandate control further proceedings. Ordinary fact, defense and remedial work must respect the supported record rather than manufacture new justification.

### Sterling v. Borough of Minersville — 2001-04-24

Authority: [controlling holdings](../records/Sterling_v_Borough_of_Minersville_merits_2001-04-24.md#holdings).

#### I. An operative official ultimatum can invade intimate confidentiality

**Holding and operative rule:** An officer violates the Fourteenth Amendment protection of intimate confidentiality by imposing a credible official ultimatum requiring an adult to disclose suspected sexual orientation to a family member or face disclosure by the officer, when no legitimate governmental purpose supports either course. The present infringement is operative compelled surrender of an objectively intimate confidence; completed officer disclosure is unnecessary, but an idle insult or nonoperative threat does not satisfy this rule.

**Authority:** Part I of Souter's opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer. Those seven directly adopt the right, application on the supported assumptions and stated limits.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an officer cannot evade protected confidentiality by making the citizen perform the disclosure. Sheets protects objectively intimate marital and family information against unjustified official dissemination. Denius recognizes that an official disclosure condition can itself burden intimate information before publication occurs. Today's bounded extension concerns an arrest-backed command to reveal an adult's intimate life to family, rather than every threat or a general right to keep all information secret.

The supported account describes an operative choice imposed through official arrest authority: Wayman must disclose his suspected orientation or the officer will disclose it. The alleged demand therefore does more than predict possible future misconduct. Its coercion presently targets the protected confidence. No supported drinking-enforcement purpose connects disclosure to his grandfather with this adult's arrest. Religious admonition supplies no policing justification. Disputed admissions do not establish that the information was already public.

Doe preserves the distinction between confined, justified benefits administration and an unrelated use; it neither requires public dissemination for every claim nor itself decides this extension. Whalen permits legitimate safeguarded collection, and Nixon recognizes personal privacy without making ownership decisive. Their foundations and the actual holdings of Sheets and Denius support this confined coercion rule, not an all-purpose informational guarantee.

The officers' strongest objection distinguishes an unexecuted threat from a completed deprivation. That distinction remains valid for an idle remark or a demand without operative effect. It does not answer a credible, arrest-backed command to surrender the confidence itself. The plaintiff must still prove the actual demand, responsible actor, coercive effect, causation and compensable injury. This legal ruling finds none of those disputed historical facts after trial.

#### II. The supported no-purpose ultimatum defeats legal immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** On the supported plaintiff-favorable account, the law effective on April 17, 1997 gave sufficiently particularized objective notice that using arrest authority to compel an adult's intimate family disclosure for no official purpose was unconstitutional; Wilinsky is not entitled to qualified immunity on that legal ground. An identical orientation-and-ultimatum case is unnecessary, but operative coercion and the complete absence of an official disclosure purpose are material limits, and subjective bad faith alone does not defeat immunity.

**Authority:** Part II of Souter's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer, supplies five direct joins to the notice ground and affirmance. O'Connor and Kennedy join the present merits rule but not this conduct-date conclusion.

**Controlling explanation:** Harlow and Anderson retain an objective, particularized notice test. Elder requires all relevant conduct-date law, not merely an identical earlier case or the authorities cited below. Sheets had taken effect in April 1996. Its completed-publication setting differs from the present ultimatum, but it established protection for objectively intimate information against exposure unrelated to the official purpose for access. The officer cannot avoid that boundary by coercing the person to perform the surrender.

The earlier Third Circuit authorities reinforce the distinction between confidential information and a genuine official need. Westinghouse addressed intimate medical material; Fraternal Order of Police addressed justified employment inquiry and disclosure safeguards; the 1995 circuit Doe opinion evaluated an actual health-plan monitoring interest. Those cases do not themselves decide this ultimatum, and their full balancing formulations are not adopted. Their justified-use boundaries, together with Sheets, make this no-purpose demand sufficiently apparent.

Walls is substantial contrary authority, but its employment-screening inquiry differs from an arrest-based command to disclose to family without a policing purpose. Decisions distinguishing ordinary threats from completed injuries likewise do not authorize operative compelled self-disclosure. The Court does not derive notice simply from the demand's offensiveness or an officer's subjective understanding.

Denius supplies present merits law, not April 1997 notice. The Supreme Court's May 1997 Doe decision, subsequent Shahar and Wilson v. Layne decisions, and still later authorities supply no retrospective notice either. The relevant cumulative law already existed when the alleged act occurred. Denial of the legal defense permits ordinary proceedings; it does not prove participation, causation of death or damages, and it orders no automatic trial.

#### III. Hoban's factual participation challenge is outside immediate review

**Holding and operative rule:** An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal cannot determine whether pretrial evidence sufficiently establishes an officer's participation in the alleged conduct. Hoban's factual-sufficiency contention is dismissed from immediate appellate review; a genuinely separable legal defense may be decided only on the proper supported assumptions, without choosing between disputed accounts.

**Authority:** Part III of Souter's opinion has all nine Justices' direct joins.

**Controlling explanation:** Johnson distinguishes immediate review of a separable legal immunity question from review of evidentiary sufficiency concerning participation. Behrens preserves that boundary within an otherwise authorized appeal; legal phrasing cannot bring the factual dispute within the appellate court's power. Hunter leaves legal immunity for judicial decision under ordinary summary-judgment constraints, and Elder requires consideration of relevant law without authorizing factual retrial. Hoban's denial of actual participation therefore does not permit appellate selection of the true account. His role remains for the proper trial-level proceedings, while any properly separable assumed-fact legal issue retains its own lawful footing.

#### Remaining limits and ordinary proceedings

The case concerns an adult and an operative no-purpose ultimatum. It decides neither an emergency protective disclosure on facts absent here nor a general right to conceal evidence of criminal conduct. No universal privacy test, strict scrutiny, least restrictive means or automatic threat liability is adopted. Whether the ultimatum occurred, each officer's responsibility, coercive effect, causation of Wayman's death, compensable injury and lawful damages remain for proof. Municipal responsibility, other constitutional claims and state-law claims retain their own predicates and actual procedural posture. Recognition of the legal rule and denial of immunity establish no damages amount or liability for every defendant.

**Precedent treatment:**

- **Sheets v. Salt Lake County, April 29, 1996:** Its objectively intimate-information protection is extended from unjustified official dissemination to the operative coerced family disclosure presented here. Its actual-publication holding remains; it did not itself decide threats. Its date makes its confined principle relevant to April 1997 notice.
- **Denius v. Dunlap, January 17, 2001:** Its recognition that a compulsory disclosure condition can impose a present burden supports the extension, with actual intimacy and official justification required. Its differentiated immunity analysis preserves conduct-date notice; its present holding supplies no earlier notice.
- **Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority, May 5, 1997:** Its assumed-right disposition of confined, justified benefits administration is preserved, distinguished from the unrelated ultimatum. It supplies no April 1997 notice. The distinct 1995 circuit opinion is relevant conduct-date authority at its own scope.
- **Whalen v. Roe; Nixon v. Administrator of General Services:** Their confidentiality foundations and approval of legitimate safeguarded governmental uses remain. Neither is treated as having decided this coercive demand.
- **Harlow v. Fitzgerald; Anderson v. Creighton:** Objective, particularized qualified immunity remains; it is neither replaced by a reliance-only defense nor defeated by subjective malice alone.
- **Elder v. Holloway; Hunter v. Bryant:** Independent consideration of relevant contemporaneous law and judicial immunity review under proper factual assumptions are applied. Neither permits appellate fact selection or retrospective notice.
- **Wilson v. Layne:** Its particular earlier notice determination and rejection of a universal identical-case requirement remain. Its 1999 decision is not itself April 1997 notice.
- **Johnson v. Jones; Behrens v. Pelletier:** Their factual-sufficiency exclusion and distinct assumed-fact legal-review channel govern Hoban's contention.
- **Westinghouse; Fraternal Order of Police; the 1995 circuit Doe opinion:** Considered as preconduct circuit confidentiality authorities concerning actual official functions and safeguards; they are not elevated into Supreme Court holdings or transplanted wholesale.
- **Walls v. City of Petersburg:** Its employment-inquiry setting and contrary notice significance are considered and distinguished from this no-purpose arrest-backed family disclosure demand; its existence is not ignored.

**Resulting law:** Protected intimate confidentiality reaches the credible official ultimatum compelling an adult's family disclosure without legitimate governmental purpose.
